"You're allowed to be nervous," Buffy told him, the night before, both of them sitting on the porch steps the way they usually ended up when something needed saying carefully. "This is enormous. Nervous is the correct response to enormous."

"I know. I just wish enormous came with better instructions."

"Nothing enormous ever does. You just kind of have to walk into it and figure out the rest as you go."

"That's not super comforting."

"It's not meant to be comforting. It's meant to be true." She bumped his shoulder with hers. "And I need you to actually hear this part — whatever happens tomorrow, you're not walking into it broken or lost or missing anything. You're walking into it as somebody who's spent four years doing something actually remarkable, alone, and came out the other side of that still kind, still funny, still exactly who you were before any of this started. That's not nothing. That's actually kind of amazing, if you think about it."

He looked at her for a long moment, something in his chest loosening slightly at the words, and kissed her, slow and grateful, the kind of kiss that said more than the actual words had room for.

"Thanks. I needed that more than I probably let on."

"I know. That's why I said it."

He jumped the next morning carrying considerably more than his usual pack — nerves, hope, four years of believing he was the only person alive who'd ever felt this particular pull, all riding on whether this jump, out of however many he had left in him, would actually drop him back at the one fence line he'd once found by accident, ready, finally, to approach it on purpose if it did.

✳

89.5. Cent: "He's coming."

Breakfast, and I nearly choked on cereal getting the words out.

"He's coming. This trip. He said so."

Mom set her mug down carefully. "When?"

"Doesn't say exactly. Soon."

Dad looked at me a long moment. "You ready?"

I thought about four years of somebody else being alone with this, about to not be, because of a piece of carved wood and a stubborn seventeen-year-old who wouldn't let a boot print go unexplained.

"Yeah," I said. "I think I actually am."

89.6. Millie: The Argument

I found Davy in the kitchen, staring at the note Cent had left on the counter — He's coming this trip — like it might rearrange itself into something less alarming if he looked at it long enough.

"You're doing the face," I said.

"I don't have a face."

"You have several faces. This is the one from the anniversary of the thing you won't call by its name. Also known as your only face, most days."

That got the corner of his mouth, briefly, before it went serious again. "A stranger. On our land. That we found out about because Cent went looking for footprints instead of telling us the second she suspected something."

"She told us the second she was sure. That's not the same as hiding it."

"It's close enough that I don't love it."

"I know you don't. I don't fully love it either." I pulled out the chair across from him. "But you're not actually arguing about Cent right now."

"I'm not?"

"You're arguing about Conley's basement."

He went still, the old flinch, quick and small. "That's not — " He stopped. Tried again, quieter. "That's not fair."

"It's completely fair, and you know it, and I'm allowed to say it because I was the one who spent that whole stretch not knowing if you were even alive." I didn't soften it. Didn't need to, not with him. "That's not a scar that heals into nothing."

"So you agree with me."

"I agree the fear's real. I don't agree it gets to make this decision by itself."

"Why not? It's kept us alive this long."

"It's kept us careful. Careful and controlling aren't the same animal, even if they wear the same coat." I reached for his hand, didn't quite get it yet. "If we clamp down on her now, for a reason that's about us and not about him, she learns the caution was never really about safety."

"What if he's exactly the kind of thing the people who took me would send? Patient. Careful. Feels harmless right up until —"

"Then we watch. We have a plan. We trust her read, because she's had a better one than either of us managed at seventeen." I got the hand this time. "You taught her to run. Trust that it took."

"I taught her to run from people who mean her harm. I didn't teach her what it looks like when somebody's just lonely." A beat. "I was that lonely once. Before you. Didn't stop them finding a use for it."

"Maybe that's the actual lesson, then. Not danger versus safety. Lonely versus dangerous. They're not the same thing, and you of all people know the difference better than anybody."

He turned his coffee cup slowly, didn't drink from it. "I still don't love it."

"You don't have to love it. You just can't stand in front of it." I squeezed once. "We'll be close. Not close enough to hover. Close enough to help."

"That's the whole plan? Hope really hard and stand nearby?"

"That's most of parenting, Davy. I don't know why you're only noticing now."

He almost laughed at that, the sound coming out rougher than it should have, and finally nodded, the worry still sitting there under it, the way it always did when Conley's name got this close to the surface. "Okay. Her call. We back her play."

"We back her play," I agreed, and neither of us let go of the other's hand for a while after that.

✳

Chapter 90: Chapter 90

The jump dropped him in the usual spot, and Xander stood there a long moment just breathing, gathering whatever nerve he had left before approaching the fence line one more time.

She was already waiting.

Not hiding, not watching from a careful distance the way he'd half-expected — just standing near the marker, arms crossed loosely, dark hair pulled back, dressed like someone who spent a lot of time outdoors and had stopped caring what that looked like a long time ago. She saw him the same instant he saw her, and neither of them moved for a second, the whole moment stretching out longer than it probably actually lasted.

"Okay," Xander said, because his mouth apparently needed to say something even if his brain hadn't caught up yet. "Okay, this is really happening. You're real. You're an actual person."

"Yeah," she said, and her voice cracked slightly on the single word, which made him feel marginally less insane for how unsteady his own felt. "You too. Xander, right?"

"Xander. Yeah. And you're Cent."

"Cent." She took a step closer, then another, like she was testing whether the ground between them was actually safe to cross, which he supposed, in a very literal sense, it was. "I've been trying to figure out what to say for like a week and I've got nothing. Every version sounded stupid out loud."

"I had the exact same problem. I think I rehearsed six different opening lines and forgot every single one the second I saw you standing there."

"What was your best one?"

"Honestly? 'Hi.' Just hi. I peaked with the dirt-scratched version, apparently, and everything since has been a downgrade."

That got a real laugh out of her, short and surprised, like it had escaped before she'd fully decided to let it. "That's fair. Mine wasn't much better. I had a whole speech about solidarity and shared experience and it just — vanished."

"Solidarity and shared experience. That's a good speech. I'm sorry it vanished."

"I can probably reconstruct it if you're really that invested."

"I am extremely invested in hearing your solidarity speech."

She laughed again, and something in her shoulders eased, the careful distance between them shrinking without either of them making a visible decision about it.

"Four years," she said finally, quieter. "That's what you wrote. Four years alone."

"Yeah." He didn't try to make it sound lighter than it was. "You?"

"I've had it my whole life, basically. Family thing — I mean, not hereditary exactly, we're still working out what it actually is, but I grew up with two parents who could do this too. Never had to figure it out by myself."

"That sounds so much better than how I did it."

"Sounds lonelier, how you did it."

"Yeah. It was." He met her eyes. "Not anymore, though. I'm kind of hoping that part's over now."

"I think that part's over," Cent said. "I really do."

They stood there another moment, the fence line between their two worlds no longer feeling like the border it had once seemed, and Xander found himself smiling — really, fully, in a way that had nothing forced about it.

"So," he said. "What now? Because I've got approximately zero protocol for this part."

"Same. Completely making it up as I go." She glanced back toward the distant farmhouse. "You want to actually come inside? Meet my parents properly? Or is that too much too fast?"

He considered it, the size of the invitation, everything it implied about trust already extended on both sides.

"Yeah," he said. "I think I'm ready for that too."

She grinned, and it was, he thought, a truly good grin, the kind that made the whole enormous, terrifying, four-years-in-the-making moment feel suddenly, wonderfully manageable.

"Come on then," Cent said. "Let's go tell my parents the door actually opened all the way."

✳

Chapter 91: Chapter 91

I got there early. Paced. Made myself stop pacing. Started again anyway.

He showed up right on time, which I hadn't expected—figured someone doing this alone for four years might run late out of pure nerves, but no, dead on time, standing there looking exactly as freaked out as I felt.

Good. Made it fair.

"Okay," he said. First word. "Okay, this is really happening. You're real. You're an actual person."

"Yeah." My voice did something embarrassing on the word. Didn't care. "You too. Xander, right?"

"Xander. Yeah. And you're Cent."

Hearing him say it back—my actual name, not a signature on a note—did something to my chest I wasn't fully braced for.

We did the awkward thing where neither of us knew what came next. I'd had a whole speech ready, something about solidarity, about shared experience, something that sounded good in my head a week ago and turned to absolutely nothing the second he was standing there in front of me.

"I had a speech," I admitted. "It's gone. Completely vanished."

"Same. I had six opening lines. Forgot all of them. Best I've got is 'hi,' and I already used that one, on the dirt."

That got a laugh out of me before I meant to give him one. Good laugh, though. Real.

"Four years," I said, because it kept sitting in my chest and I needed to say it out loud to him instead of just to myself. "Alone the whole time."

"Yeah." He didn't dress it up. I liked that.

"I got mine at fifteen. Avalanche, if you can believe that—snowboarding accident, buried under about two yards of snow, and then I wasn't. Scared me worse than the avalanche did, honestly. But I grew up around two parents who already did it, so at least I had people who knew what was happening to me the second it happened. Didn't have to figure out the how by myself. Just the why."

"That sounds so much better than how I did it."

"Sounds lonelier, how you did it."

"Yeah. It was." He looked at me steady. "Not anymore, though. Hoping that part's over."

"I think it is," I said, and meant it more than I'd meant almost anything in longer than I wanted to admit.

We stood there a while, not saying much, just letting the fact of each other settle in properly. Then I asked if he wanted to actually come meet Mom and Dad, and he said yes without much hesitation, which told me something good about him before he'd even said another word.

I walked him back toward the house. Four years of somebody else's alone, about to end. Easily the strangest and best thing I'd ever helped set in motion. I kept thinking that, the whole walk back.

Dad was on the porch when we came into view. Saw us coming. Stood up slow, the way he does when something matters.

"Dad," I called out. "This is Xander."

He came down the steps, hand already out, and I watched Xander's face do the thing mine probably did the first time somebody who actually understood looked at me like I wasn't strange for existing.

Good start, I thought. Really good start.

✳

Chapter 92: Chapter 92

Davy's handshake was firmer than Xander expected, and steadier, like a man who'd made a point of never letting anyone see his hand shake even when it probably wanted to.

"Xander," Davy said, testing the name the way Cent had said she would. "Good to finally put a face to the boot prints."

"Good to finally have a face to put. Sorry about the boot prints, honestly. Wasn't trying to be mysterious, I just genuinely didn't know anybody else existed to notice them."

"No apology needed. You handled finding us about as well as anybody could." Davy's eyes did a quick, practiced sweep — not unfriendly, just thorough, the look of a man who'd learned the hard way to actually look at people instead of just seeing them. "Cent tells us you've been doing this alone a long time."

"Four years. Give or take a lifetime, some days."

Something crossed Davy's face at that, brief and complicated, gone before Xander could fully read it. "I know something about that certain flavor of alone. Different circumstances. Same shape."

A woman appeared in the doorway then — dark hair, sharp eyes, the kind of calm that Xander immediately recognized as the load-bearing kind, the sort that came from years of practice staying steady while everyone around her wasn't. "You must be Xander," she said. "I'm Millie. I'd say welcome, but I think Cent already covered that part rather thoroughly, with an actual carved sign."

"She did. Best welcome I've gotten in four years, easily."

"Low bar, from what I understand," Millie said, though there was warmth under it, not judgment. "Come in. Sit. You've had a long few weeks getting here, I imagine, even if it only took you a few trips to actually arrive."

The kitchen was warmer than Xander expected, wood-heated and lived-in, and he found himself sitting at a table that had clearly hosted a thousand ordinary family dinners despite everything extraordinary about the family eating them.

"So," Davy said, settling across from him. "Four years. No family who knew. How'd you figure out the rules on your own?"

"Badly, mostly. Trial and error. A lot of error." Xander felt himself relax slightly under the question, honestly relieved to finally explain the shape of his last four years to somebody who'd actually understand the answer. "I learned the hard way that you can't control where you land. Learned the hard way about a few dozen things, honestly."

"We might be able to help with some of that," Millie said. "Assuming you want the help."

"I very much want the help."

Davy and Millie exchanged a look — quick, wordless, the kind of look that clearly carried an entire conversation neither of them needed to say out loud — and something in it settled whatever last worry Davy had been carrying into the room.

"Then let's get you fed first," Millie said, already standing. "Everything else can wait until you're not running on four years of granola bars and nerves."

"That's..." Xander considered the offer, something loosening in his chest that had been tight for longer than just the walk up to the porch. "That actually sounds incredible, honestly."

"Good," Millie said. "Sit tight. This part, at least, is easy."

✳

Chapter 93: Chapter 93

Dad's initial read on him lasted about four seconds, but I've watched that sweep enough times to clock it happening even when he thinks he's being subtle. He's not, for the record. Never has been.

Xander passed it. I could tell by how fast Dad's shoulders came down.

Mom had him fed within twenty minutes. Her version of the same test. You learn a lot watching how somebody acts around food they didn't have to ask for. Xander said thank you like he meant it. Ate like he hadn't in days. Apologized once for "being kind of a lot" when he got excited describing some bird he'd photographed on another world. Not a critique he was getting in this house, of all houses.

"You're not a lot," I told him. "You're barely a lot. I've met actual a-lots. This isn't that."

"Comforting. Extremely comforting to be ranked."

"You're welcome."

Dad watched the whole exchange with a look I know well by now — not suspicion, the other one, the one that means he's recalculating something.

Later, once Xander had gone quiet, just eating, just being in the room without performing anything for anybody, Dad caught my eye across the table and gave me the smallest nod. Just once. I knew exactly what it meant.

He's real. He's exactly what he said he was. You called it right.

I didn't say anything back. Just held the look a second. Some things don't need words between us. Four years of him teaching me to read people, finally paying off in a way that actually mattered.

Mom asked him about the sloth. Of course she did. Word travels, even across worlds, apparently, once Xander starts talking about anything he's proud of.

"There's a sloth," he confirmed. "Car-sized. I have photos. I will absolutely show you all of them if given even the slightest encouragement."

"Consider yourself encouraged," Mom said. That was that. The whole rest of the afternoon disappeared into sloth photos and bird measurements — four years of somebody else's collected wonder finally getting an audience big enough to hold it.

I caught myself just watching him talk at one point. Animated. Genuinely happy, in a way I don't think he'd let himself be in front of strangers in longer than he'd probably admit. Something settled in my own chest. Didn't even realize it had been unsettled until right then.

This is going to be good, I thought. Whatever this turns into. This is going to be good.

✳

Chapter 94: Chapter 94

"So it went well," Buffy said, the second Xander walked through the door glowing like a man who'd had an actually good day for once, instead of a merely survived one, already crossing the room to hug him properly before he'd even gotten his jacket off.

"It went really well," he said, into her hair, holding on a beat longer than a normal hello usually warranted. "Like, better than I let myself hope for. Her parents are great. Actual great, not just polite great."

Buffy pulled back enough to kiss him, quick and really pleased, before finally letting him go. "I'm so glad."

"Tell us everything," Willow said, already pulling out a chair like she was settling in for a full debrief, which she absolutely was.

He told them everything — Davy's handshake, Millie's cooking, the sloth photos, the plain relief of finally being somewhere that understood the shape of his last four years without him having to explain any of it from scratch.

"They know things," he said. "Real things, techniques, stuff I've been doing badly by accident that apparently has an actual right way to do it. Millie offered to help me figure some of it out."

"That's huge," Willow said. "Xander, that's genuinely huge. Four years of trial and error, and there's just — an instruction manual, basically, that you never had access to."

"Feels like that, yeah. A little overwhelming, honestly, realizing how much I've been doing the hard way that didn't have to be hard."

"Don't beat yourself up about it," Buffy said. "You didn't have the instruction manual. You did the best anybody could with what you had."

"I know. Still weird, though. Like finding out there was a shortcut the whole time."

Faith, who'd been quiet through most of the debrief, finally spoke up. "I get that feeling, actually. Found out a few months back that stuff I'd been white-knuckling through my whole life had actual names, actual explanations. Doesn't make the years before feel wasted, exactly. Just makes you wish you'd known sooner."

"Yeah," Xander said. "That's exactly it."

"You'll get there faster now, though. That's the upside." Faith stole a fry off his plate, on principle. "Sounds like a good family. Good instincts, if they took to you that fast."

"Really good instincts. Her dad clocked me in about four seconds, apparently. I didn't even notice it happening until Cent told me about it after."

"That's the good kind of clocking," Giles said, having arrived partway through and caught enough of the story to weigh in. "The kind that comes from genuine care rather than mere suspicion. I'm glad to hear it, Xander. Genuinely."

"Me too," Xander said, and meant it more than he'd expected to say out loud, four years of believing he was the only one who could do this finally, properly, no longer true at all.

✳

94.5. The Wolf

The dire wolves had been sitting in the back of his mind for weeks, filed alongside the mammoth and whatever had made those punctures in the kill site, waiting on the "proper planning" Giles had insisted on. Proper planning, it turned out, mostly meant Millie.

"You want to dart one of the pack animals," she said, when he finally worked up the nerve to actually ask, not a question so much as a confirmation of something she'd clearly already guessed was coming. "Smart to start there instead of the big tusked ones or the cats. Wolves you can read. Pack behavior's honest, once you know what you're looking at."

"Honest how?"

"They tell you what they're gonna do before they do it. Ears, tail, weight shift. The two you're worried about don't give you nearly that much warning." She pulled a battered notebook off a shelf that looked like it had survived much worse than a Sunnydale kitchen. "Weight estimate's the hard part. Get that wrong and you either don't drop him fast enough or you drop him too hard. I've got rough numbers from Dad's old notes. We'll refine them once you've actually got eyes on the pack again."

Willow came along for the dosage math, notebook already covered in her own cross-referenced calculations by the time they actually made the jump, and Buffy came for exactly the reason Buffy always came on the dangerous ones — because she wasn't about to let Xander go stand near a pack of oversized wolves without backup.

The jump dropped all three of them a cautious distance from where the game trail had been, and they spent the better part of the first day simply relocating the pack, moving slow and quiet, letting Xander's four years of fieldwork do the work Millie's notes couldn't.

"There," Buffy breathed, the second afternoon, pointing toward a rise where several dark shapes moved with an easy, coordinated patience that made the hair on Xander's arms stand up despite the warm weather. "Okay. Those are wolves the way a bus is a car."

"Big," Willow agreed, already doing math against her notebook. "Bigger than my estimate, actually. Recalculating."

They picked the straggler — an older animal, trailing the pack's edge, moving with the particular deliberateness of something that had learned not to waste effort it didn't have to spend. Xander got close on his stomach through knee-high grass, closer than any sense of self-preservation strictly recommended, Willow's revised number running through his head the whole crawl.

The dart went in clean. The wolf's head came up fast, a low sound building in its chest that had all three of them go completely still, but the pack — reading something in its posture that Xander couldn't, exactly what Millie had meant by honest — didn't scatter, didn't charge, just watched, wary and alert, while their packmate's legs slowly stopped cooperating with the rest of it.

"Okay," Xander said, once it had gone down and stayed down, breathing slow and even. "Okay, that actually worked."

"That actually worked," Willow confirmed, sounding almost as surprised as he felt.

They didn't linger to admire it. Alan's team — briefed this time, truly briefed, not scrambling after the fact — had a notably larger transport crate waiting when the pull brought all three of them and one very confused, very sedated dire wolf home, and Millie's numbers, it turned out, had been close enough to right that Alan spent most of the debrief asking politely insistent questions about where, exactly, Xander had gotten such good dosage data.

"A friend," Xander said, which was true, and left it there.

✳

Chapter 95: Chapter 95

This one he'd deliberately gone alone — new technique, first real test of it, and some part of him had wanted to prove it out solo before trusting it with anyone else riding along.

The world this time was warm and wet, thick jungle canopy dripping steadily from a rain that had only just stopped, and Xander found himself testing something new before he'd even gotten his bearings — a technique Millie had walked him through the week before, a way of steadying a fall that didn't require the blind luck he'd relied on for four years.

"Okay," he said, working through the mental checklist she'd given him. "Controlled descent, not just controlled panic. Big difference, apparently."

He practiced it twice, deliberately, on a low branch that wouldn't hurt much if he got it wrong, and found, to his own genuine surprise, that it worked exactly the way she'd described.

"Huh," he said, grinning despite himself. "Huh, that's — that's actually so much better than how I've been doing it."

The rest of the trip felt different in a hundred small ways, technique layered under experience that had already been solid, just less efficient than it needed to be. He moved through the canopy with more confidence, tested his footing with a method that actually made sense instead of just crossed fingers, and found himself, more than once, wishing he could tell past-him that help had been coming, eventually, if he could just hang on a little longer.

'This is what four years should've felt like,' he thought, working his way carefully down toward a stream he could hear but not yet see. 'Not easier exactly. Just less like guessing.' It was a good feeling, honestly good, the kind that made even an ordinary jump feel like a small victory instead of just another routine outing.

He found a troop of monkeys midafternoon, small and quick and entirely unbothered by his presence, chattering at each other in a rhythm that felt almost like conversation, and spent a solid hour just documenting them with the same careful patience he'd always brought to this part of the job, technique or no technique.

By the time the jump pulled him home, he had monkey photos, an actually improved landing technique, and a quiet satisfaction that came easy after a trip that had gone well in ways both old and totally new.

"How was the jungle," Willow asked, the second he walked in.

"Good. Really good, actually. Tried the landing thing Millie showed me. Worked perfectly."

"You're gonna have to actually teach me that one, since apparently my chart needs a whole new column for post-training incident rates."

"Deal," Xander said, already pulling out his notebook. "Prepare to be very disappointed by how boring the data's about to get."

"I've never been so excited for boring data in my life."

✳

Chapter 96: Chapter 96

Willow's excitement about Xander's new landing technique lasted about a day before it evolved into something significantly bigger, the way most of her excitements eventually did.

She found him at his locker the way she always did before first period, squeezing in against his side while he dug through the mess of notebooks and loose papers, one arm looping around his waist without either of them registering it as a decision anymore, and stayed there, half-blocking the run of lockers behind them, entirely unbothered by the stream of students detouring around the two of them to get to class.

"Okay, I need to ask something, and I need you to not think I'm being weird about it," she said, catching Xander at his locker before first period.

"You're always a little weird about things. I've made my peace with it." He didn't look up from the locker, arm settling around her shoulders in the same automatic way, the two of them folding into each other in the middle of the hallway the way they'd apparently stopped noticing they did.

"Fair. Anyway — Cent's parents. They've been doing this for decades, right? Actual decades of data, actual understanding of how the ability works, actual documented technique instead of just trial and error."

"That's basically what I said too, yeah."

"Do you think they'd talk to me? Not about anything personal, obviously, I know there's a lot they probably can't or won't share. Just — the mechanics. How it actually works, physically, whatever they're comfortable explaining."

Xander considered that, weighing it seriously rather than brushing it off. "I can ask. Can't promise anything, given everything they've been through. But I think Millie especially might actually enjoy it. She seemed like the kind of person who's spent a long time not having anybody to nerd out with about this stuff either."

"I would be so respectful. I would bring actual prepared questions. I would not be weird about it, or at least, I would contain the weirdness to acceptable levels."

"That's the most reasonable promise I've ever heard you make."

"I'm capable of reasonable! Occasionally!"

He laughed, genuinely, and found himself already composing the request in his head — careful, respectful, giving them every opening to say no without it costing anything between them. "I'll ask this weekend. No promises, but I think it's worth asking."

"Thank you. Genuinely. This could be — Xander, this could be actual groundbreaking stuff, if they're willing to share even a fraction of what they must know."

"I know. That's part of why I want to ask carefully. Feels like a big thing to request from people who've spent their whole lives being careful about exactly this kind of exposure."

Willow sobered slightly at that, catching the weight of it. "You're right. I don't want to push if it's not okay."

"I don't think you're pushing. I think you're asking, which is different, and I think they'll appreciate the distinction if it comes to that." He closed his locker. "Worst case, they say no, and we're exactly where we already were. Best case, you get your groundbreaking stuff and probably a new friend to nerd out with."

"I would like the best case, please."

"I'll do what I can," Xander said, and meant it. The bell rang, and neither of them moved right away, still tangled together against the lockers while the hallway emptied out around them, the specific reluctance of two people who apparently found forty-five minutes apart a really difficult prospect. "I gotta go, I gotta go," he said, not going, kissing her once, then again when the first one didn't seem to take.

"You have Simmons for first period, you're literally three doors down."

"Three doors is a lot of doors, Willow."

"Go," she said, laughing, giving him one more shove toward his classroom door, and he went, backward, still looking at her until he nearly walked into a freshman, already looking forward to the conversation despite the size of what he was about to ask.

✳

96.5. Cent: "Your friend wants to study us?"

Xander asked it careful, the way he'd started asking most things about my family lately, like he was still testing how much weight the ground could take before it gave.

"Not study like a lab rat. Study like — she's smart, truly smart, and she's spent months chasing half-answers about how this works. Your mom's the first real answer she's ever had access to."

"And you trust her."

"Completely. She's careful with things that matter. I've watched her be careful with plenty."

I turned that over, standing there at the fence with him. Mom would decide for herself regardless of what I thought, but he'd asked me first, which counted for something.

"Ask her," I said. "Mom, I mean. Not me. It's her call, and she'll actually consider it instead of just reacting."

"That's what I figured. Just wanted your read first."

"My read's that your friend sounds like somebody I'd probably like. Anybody who chases a mystery that hard usually turns out interesting."

"She is extremely interesting. Occasionally exhausting. Mostly interesting."

I laughed at that, actually laughed, the kind that surprised me a little given how careful I'd been keeping myself about all of this. "Sounds like most of the good people I know."

"Takes one to know one."

"Rude. Accurate. Rude."

Mom said yes before he left that trip, with conditions, same as she does everything, and the actual meeting happened his next time through. I wasn't there for it — didn't need to be, this one wasn't mine to sit in on — but I got the full report that evening at the fence line, Xander practically vibrating about how well it had gone.

"She's amazing," he said. "Your mom. Willow's amazing. It was a honestly great two hours."

"Told you she'd be worth it."

"You called it. I'm formally acknowledging you called it."

"Get it in writing next time. I like proof."

"Noted. I'll bring a notarized statement to the next visit."

I walked back to the house smiling, something easy sitting in my chest that hadn't been there a few months ago, back when all I had was a boot print and a hope I hadn't let myself trust yet.

✳

96.7. The Mastodon Problem

"Okay, so, bigger problem than the wolf," Xander said, dropping into the kitchen chair across from Willow's spread of notes, visibly bursting to deliver a very large realization. "The mastodon. We're doing this. I just need everybody to understand it's not gonna be quick."

"Or mammoth. We still don't know which." Willow didn't look up from her calculations. "Also, what kind of bigger problem?"

"The kind where I did actual math with Alan this morning, not just vibes, and the honest answer is: nothing we currently own can safely move something that size. Not the trailer, not the enclosure budget, none of it. We'd need custom equipment, a specialized large-mammal vet, probably a crane, and about four times what the sloth cost, which was already apparently a stretch." Xander sat down heavily. "So it's happening. It's just happening on Alan's timeline, not mine, and his timeline currently says weeks, not days."

"Weeks," Willow repeated, weighing it. "I can live with weeks. Weeks is a lot better than never."

"Millie's already reaching out to a couple of people her dad used to work with, actual large-animal specialists, since apparently four years of amateur enthusiasm doesn't quite cover 'multi-ton Ice Age herbivore.' Alan's drafting a bigger enclosure plan in parallel so we're not starting from scratch once the transport side's solved." He managed a tired grin. "Turns out there's a size where 'bring it home' stops being an adventure and starts being an actual engineering project."

Willow considered that, tapping her pen against the table. "I can work with an engineering project. Honestly, knowing it's happening, even slowly, is a lot better than the alternative. And it doesn't slow down the other one."

"The other one," Xander agreed, with slightly less enthusiasm than the sentence probably deserved. "Right. The sabertooth. Which you're still absolutely set on, once the mastodon's actually handled."

"Especially the sabertooth," Willow confirmed, utterly unrepentant. "One thing at a time, though. Mastodon first, since it's already in motion. Some things are worth doing right, even if 'right' takes longer."

"That's gonna be on my tombstone. 'He tried to do it right, eventually, on a schedule Alan controlled.'"

"Better than 'he winged it and got eaten.'"

"Fair. Genuinely fair."

✳

Chapter 97: Chapter 97

The symbol Faith had spent months chasing sat quiet in Giles' back cabinet now, contained and studied and no longer urgent, and she found she genuinely didn't know what to do with an evening that didn't have some version of that mystery hanging over it.

"Feels weird," she told Buffy, both of them walking their usual patrol route without much urgency behind it. "Having actual free time. I've kind of forgotten how it works."

"You could take up a hobby. Something that isn't monitoring an ancient summoning circle."

"What, like knitting?"

"I was thinking more like reading for fun. Or sleeping in. Wild stuff, I know."

"Sleeping in sounds deeply suspicious, but I'll consider it." Faith stretched, working out a knot from a week that had, for once, been actually ordinary. "You ever think it's weird, how much of both our lives is just waiting for the next thing to go wrong?"

"Constantly. Mostly I try not to think about it too hard, because then I start wondering what I'd even do with myself if it ever actually stopped."

"Same. Scary thought, honestly. Being bored sounds nice right up until you imagine actually living it."

They walked the rest of the route in comfortable quiet, the ordinary rhythm of a slow patrol night settling in, no countdown, no cambion, no mystery mural waiting to be decoded.

"How's Xander's whole thing going?" Faith asked, eventually. "The other jumper family."

"Really well, from what he's said. Her parents seem solid. He's already picking up new techniques, according to Willow, who I think is dangerously close to asking if she can move in with them for research purposes."

"That tracks. Give her a real scientific mystery and she loses all sense of boundaries."

"In the most loving way possible."

"Obviously." Faith looked up at the sky, clear tonight, more stars visible than usual through the streetlight glare. "Good for him, though. Four years is a long time to carry something alone. Glad he's got people now who actually get it."

"Yeah," Buffy said. "Me too."

They finished the route without incident, the whole night fully uneventful in the best possible way, and Faith found herself, walking home afterward, really enjoying just how good it felt to have a problem actually finish being a problem, at least for now.

She still stopped by Buffy's room before turning in, same quiet ritual it had become without either of them ever really naming it, and gave the hammer's handle one more try in the dark. Still nothing — and this time she actually huffed out something close to a laugh, short and rueful, shaking her head at her own reflection like she'd just lost a bet she'd known better than to make. She was starting to wonder, some nights, if nothing was just going to be the permanent answer. The wondering didn't sting quite as much as it used to. That felt, in its own strange way, like its own kind of answer, even if it wasn't the one she'd actually been asking for.

✳

Chapter 98: Chapter 98

Millie said yes, with conditions, which Xander relayed back to Willow clearly relieved, since he'd been more nervous asking than he'd let on.

"No names, no locations, nothing that could trace back to them specifically. And she wants to meet you first, before any actual science happens. Her rules, not mine."

"Completely fair. More than fair. I'll bring a list of questions and she can veto whatever she wants."

The meeting happened on neutral ground, a diner two towns over that Xander picked specifically because it wasn't anywhere either family called home, and Willow spent the first ten minutes so carefully polite that Xander nearly laughed watching her restrain herself.

"You can ask your questions," Millie said, finally, amused. "I promise I won't bite. I've had four years less practice than your friend here at being underwhelmed by teenagers with big scientific ambitions, but I manage."

Willow visibly relaxed. "Okay. First question, and feel free to just say pass if it's too much — is the ability itself measurable? Like, is there a physical marker, something in the body that changes, or is it purely — I don't even know what the alternative would be. Purely something else?"

"Good question, and an honest answer is we don't fully know. There are theories. Some kind of neurological pathway that activates under specific conditions, most commonly extreme stress, though that's clearly not universal, given how it's shown up in people who never had that particular trigger."

"Extreme stress being the common trigger, though. That tracks with what Xander's told me about his own start."

"It does. It's the most consistent pattern we've found, for whatever that's worth." Millie studied Willow for a moment, something considering in her expression. "You're asking good questions. Careful ones. I appreciate that more than you probably realize."

"I've had a lot of practice being careful with things that matter," Willow said. "This clearly matters."

They talked for two hours, Willow's careful list of questions slowly giving way to genuine, easy conversation, and by the time they left, Millie had agreed to a second meeting, and Willow had a notebook full of careful, hedged, truly groundbreaking information that she promised, several times, completely unprompted, never to misuse.

"That went well," Xander said, once they were walking back to the car.

"That went incredibly well. Xander, she basically confirmed a theory I've had for months. This is — I don't even have words for how big this actually is."

"I'm glad. Genuinely. You've earned this one."

"We've earned this one," Willow corrected. "You're the one who asked. I just showed up with a notebook."

"A very good notebook."

"The best notebook," Willow agreed, and he kissed her temple as they walked, both of them settling into the easy, satisfied quiet of people who'd both gotten something they badly needed, in completely different ways, from the exact same afternoon.

✳

99. Millie: Settling

Millie found Davy on the porch after Xander's third visit, watching the tree line with an expression she hadn't seen on him in longer than she could easily place — not worry, for once. Something closer to peace.

"You're not doing the face," she said, sitting down beside him.

"I don't have a face."

"You have several faces, and none of them are showing up tonight. It's unsettling."

"Should I manufacture one? For your comfort?"

"Please don't. I've earned this one quiet evening." She watched the same tree line he was watching. "He's good for Cent. You've seen that as clearly as I have."

"I have. Doesn't stop the part of me that's counting exits every time he jumps in."

"That part of you counts exits at the grocery store, Davy. It's not a Xander thing."

"Fair. Though in my defense, the grocery store has genuinely bad sightlines near the dairy case."

"You've said that. Repeatedly. To the manager, once, which I still apologize for on your behalf whenever I run into him."

He almost smiled at that. "He took it well."

"He did not take it well. He gave me a coupon and avoided eye contact for a month." She bumped his shoulder. "I like him too, you know. Xander."

"I know you do. You fed him within twenty minutes. That's your version of a background check."

"It's an excellent background check. Tells you everything about how somebody handles being given something they didn't ask for."

"And?"

"He passed. Obviously. You already know he passed, or you wouldn't be sitting out here looking like this instead of running your usual three-day surveillance protocol."

Davy huffed something that was almost a laugh. "I did consider the surveillance protocol."

"I know you did. I found your notes."

"They were good notes."

"They were unnecessary notes, and I burned them, and you're welcome." She took his hand anyway, same gesture, same meaning it always carried. "I think this one's good, Davy. One you don't have to spend years bracing against."

"I hope so." A beat. "I'll still probably watch the dairy case, though."

"Obviously. I'd worry if you didn't."

They sat there a while longer, no urgency to move, nothing waiting past the tree line for once, and Millie let herself enjoy exactly that — an evening with nothing to brace against, sitting next to a man who'd earned, however grudgingly, the right to just let something be good.

✳

99.5. The Mastodon

It took Alan the better part of three weeks to actually assemble what he needed — a specialized crane rig borrowed from a rescue outfit two states over, a large-mammal veterinarian Millie's old contact had vouched for personally, a trailer reinforced well past anything the zoo had ever owned before, and an enclosure crew working double shifts on foundations that had to go in before anyone dared bring the animal home to them. None of it could actually cross with him — no jump was going to carry a crane truck, and the wildside didn't have so much as a dirt road to drive one in on regardless — so all of it stayed staged right here, waiting on whatever came back through the pull.

"This is the most expensive thing I've ever been involved in," Xander said, watching the crew run a final check on the crane before he and Buffy headed for the shed. "I want that on the record. Mayor Wilkins is gonna need a bigger plaque budget."

"Mayor Wilkins already approved a bigger plaque budget," Alan said, without looking up from his clipboard. "He said, and I quote, 'if the boy's found us a mammoth, he's earned a mammoth-sized plaque.'"

Dr. Adeyemi — the large-mammal vet, sharp-eyed and completely unbothered by the scale of what she'd signed up for — had spent most of the week drilling Xander on the sedation instead of doing it herself, since there was no version of this where she could come along and administer it in person: dosage, injection site, exactly how long to wait before it was safe to get a line on the animal, all of it rehearsed until he could recite it back half-asleep, working off estimates Millie had spent just as long refining against every scrap of data her father's old notes could offer.

They found the herd on the second day, grazing along the river bottom same as Xander had seen once before, and picked the oldest bull — solitary, drifting at the herd's edge the way older males sometimes did, Millie's read on the behavior matching what little data existed almost exactly.

The dart went in clean, and then came the longest, strangest hour of Xander's entire four years, watching a creature that size slowly, ponderously, inevitably lose the fight against sedation, the herd shifting nervously at a distance but never quite committing to intervening, until the old bull's legs finally folded and the ground itself seemed to exhale.

Getting the harness under it and the lines secured after that fell to Xander and Buffy alone, working off nothing but Dr. Adeyemi's drilling and a diagram folded into the pack, every remaining minute before the timer ran out going into it.

"We are never doing that again on this tight a schedule," Xander muttered, cinching the last strap down with barely any margin left before the pull took them.

The mastodon — or mammoth, the debate still technically unresolved even now, pending an actual specialist's final word — came through the pull sedated and secured, straight into a waiting crane sling the second the shed door opened back onto solid, ordinary ground.

"Vitals are good," Dr. Adeyemi called, already moving. "Get the rig locked in, let's get him properly settled before he starts coming out of it."

It took every hand available — Alan's crew, the crane operator, Xander and Buffy still hauling cable even after everything they'd just been through — to get the animal off the harness and into the trailer, the whole operation finishing with real margin to spare now that none of it was racing a jump-timer anymore.

"Worth every bit of the three weeks," Xander said, hoarse from shouting instructions over engine noise for the better part of an hour. "Look at him."

By the time the mastodon woke groggy and confused in a purpose-built enclosure far larger than anything the zoo had built before, it was totally unharmed.

"Mammoth or mastodon?" Willow asked Dr. Adeyemi, notebook ready, the second the animal's classification became a question anyone could actually answer with confidence.

"Give me a week with proper measurements," Dr. Adeyemi said, "and I'll write you an actual paper on it."

"I would like that paper so much."

"You alright?" Buffy asked, hip bumping Xander's as they watched the crew finish securing the enclosure gate, four years of solitary jumps having never once prepared him for logistics on quite this scale.

"Yeah. Just — that was a lot. In a good way." He let out a breath he felt like he'd been holding for three weeks straight. "Two down. One much more terrifying to go."

"The sabertooth," Buffy said, not a question.

"The sabertooth," he agreed. "Willow's already got a countdown going. I've seen it. It has a name."

✳

Chapter 100: Chapter 100

Joyce's list hit a genuine milestone that Sunday, which she marked by reading the entry out loud with real ceremony: one hundred entries, and the newest one read simply, Xander found another family. A good one. I think this is the happiest I've seen him in years.

"You wrote that?" Xander asked, a little thrown by it.

"I wrote what I saw," Joyce said, passing the potatoes down the table. "You've had a different kind of light in you the last few weeks. I noticed. I write down what I notice."

"That's oddly touching for a list that started with vampire logistics."

"The list contains multitudes," Joyce said, and the table laughed harder than the joke probably deserved, the easy kind of laughter that came from an evening with nothing urgent hanging over it.

"How's Willow's research going?" Buffy asked. "The stuff with Millie."

"Incredible, from what I can tell," Xander said. "She's been buried in notes for a week. I think she's happier than I've seen her in months, honestly."

"I am extremely happy," Willow confirmed, not looking up from the notebook she'd brought to dinner despite everyone's gentle protests. "This is honestly some of the best research access I've ever had. I keep having to remind myself not to get greedy about it."

"You're allowed a little greedy," Faith said. "Given how long you've been chasing mysteries with way less to show for it."

"That's generous of you to say, but I'm trying to hold myself to some kind of standard."

"Your standard's higher than most people's greed," Jesse said. "I think you're fine."

The table settled into its usual comfortable noise after that — Faith stealing fries on principle, Jesse angling for details about Cent that Xander mostly deflected with good humor.

"Come on, is she pretty? I need visuals for my mental filing system."

"That's a weird thing to need, Jesse."

"I have a very organized brain. It requires visuals."

"She's great. That's all you're getting."

"Vague. Suspicious. I'll allow it for now, but I'm filing this under unresolved."

Joyce quietly refilling glasses, long past being surprised by any of it.

"I think that's everything," Buffy said, eventually.

"For now," Joyce echoed, fond as ever, and reached for dessert.

Later, on the porch, just Buffy and Xander, the evening quiet around them, Xander looked out at a street that felt, for the first time in longer than he could easily place, utterly uncomplicated.

"You good?" Buffy asked.

"Really good, actually. Four years of thinking I was the only person who could do this, and now I've got a whole second family who actually gets it from the inside, plus you guys, plus Willow getting to do actual groundbreaking science because of it. Feels like everything just kind of clicked into place at once."

"You earned that. All of it."

"Maybe. Feels more like I got lucky, honestly. Lucky Willow found that photo. Lucky Cent left that marker. Lucky any of it happened the way it did."

"Luck helps," Buffy agreed. "But you're the one who kept going back, four years running, even when it would've been easier not to. That's not luck. That's just you."

He considered that, something settling comfortably into place in his chest. "Yeah. Maybe both, then. Luck and me, working together for once instead of luck just happening to me."

"I'll take that," Buffy said, and leaned over to kiss him, unhurried, the two of them sitting together a while longer afterward, the ordinary Sunnydale night settling in around them, quiet and easy and, for once, entirely unremarkable in the best possible way.

✳

100.5. Cent: "Good, actually."

Dad asked me at dinner, the way he does when he actually wants an answer and not just the easy one.

"How are you doing with all this? Really doing, not the version you give when you're trying not to worry anybody."

I thought about it properly before I answered, because he'd know if I didn't.

"Good, actually. Really good." I turned my fork over, working out how to say the rest of it. "I spent a long time not knowing if there was anybody else out there like us. Now there is, and he's exactly the kind of person I'd have wanted it to be, if I'd gotten to pick."

"You did pick, a little. You're the one who left the marker."

"Fair. I picked to try. Didn't know what I'd get back."

Mom looked up from her book. "And Willow? The research?"

"Also good. Weirdly good, honestly, watching you actually get to talk shop with somebody who's spent months chasing the exact question you could just answer outright. I think you liked it as much as she did."

"I did," Mom admitted. "It's been a long time since anybody outside this family asked good questions about any of this."

"See, that's the part I didn't expect," I said. "Not just that Xander turned out okay. That this whole thing turned out to be good for all of us. Not just me getting somebody to talk to. You two getting something out of it too."

Dad reached over, squeezed my hand once, same gesture he's used my whole life for the things that actually matter. "That's usually how the good ones work. Nobody carries it alone. Everybody gets something."

"Even you? Mr. Still-Watching-The-Dairy-Case?"

"Even me," he admitted. "Though I stand by the dairy case."

"You'll always stand by the dairy case."

"It's actually bad sightlines, Cent."

I laughed, the easy kind, and looked around the table — Mom back in her book, Dad still faintly smiling, the ordinary evening settling in around a family that had, against every odd I used to quietly worry about, actually gotten to have something good land in our laps for once instead of something we had to fight for.

Good, I thought. Actually good. I'm allowed to just let that be true.

✳

100.52. The Sabertooth

Millie came herself this time.

It wasn't something Xander had expected to ask for, and it wasn't something she'd offered lightly — a full day of careful conversation with Davy first, the kind Xander only caught the edges of, before she'd finally said yes, on the condition that she called every part of it, start to finish, no exceptions.

"You've never darted a cat before," she said, checking the rifle over, unhurried, thorough, clearly having done this more times than she probably wanted to count. "Wolves warn you. Cats don't. That's not a design flaw, that's the whole design. So today, you do exactly what I say, exactly when I say it, and if I say run, you run first and ask why later."

"Understood," Xander said, and meant it more completely than he usually managed to mean anything.

Willow had run the dosage numbers three separate times, cross-referenced against everything Millie could tell her about the animal's approximate size, and had still looked faintly ill about the margin for error right up until the morning they jumped. Buffy came for backup, same as always. Faith, hearing about it secondhand, had insisted on coming too, on the grounds that "an actual sabertooth" fell well within her professional expertise as someone who fought things with teeth for a living, an argument nobody at the table had found much room to disagree with.

They found it on the second day, exactly where Millie's read on the territory had said it would be — sunning itself on a rock outcrop overlooking a game trail, tawny and enormous and, even at rest, radiating the certain stillness of something that had never once needed to hurry for anything in its life.

"Okay," Xander breathed, lying flat beside Millie in the grass, camera forgotten in his hand for once. "Okay, that's — that's a lot of cat."

"That's a lot of cat," Millie agreed, fully calm. "Wind's in our favor. Stay low, stay quiet, and let me tell you when."

The wait took most of the afternoon. Xander had never held still that long for anything in his life, not even the sloth, muscles cramping in ways he was fairly sure would still be complaining about it a week later, Faith a silent, coiled presence at his other side, watching the cat with an intensity that had nothing to do with science and everything to do with four years of trained reflex.

"Now," Millie said, quiet and certain, the second the angle opened up clean.

The dart went in high on the shoulder, and the world went loud all at once — a sound Xander felt in his chest before he consciously registered it as a roar, the sabertooth up and moving in a single fluid motion that had every one of them scrambling backward on instinct, Faith already between it and the rest of the group before anyone had decided to move.

"Hold!" Millie's voice cut through the chaos, flat and absolute. "Hold your ground, don't run, don't turn your back — it hasn't decided we're worth the effort yet, and running is the thing that decides it for him."

They held. It felt like the longest thirty seconds of Xander's life, the sabertooth's massive head swinging toward them, weighing something none of them could read, before the dart did its work and its legs started to go, the roar tapering into something lower, confused, and finally, blessedly, silent as it folded down into the grass.

Nobody moved for a long moment after that.

"Okay," Faith said, finally, voice not quite steady. "Okay, I've fought vampires, demons, a literal god's hammer situation. That's up there. That's really up there."

"You're not wrong," Xander agreed, hands shaking as he finally lowered the camera he hadn't realized he was still gripping.

Millie was already moving, checking the animal's breathing, its pulse, with the same unhurried competence she'd brought to everything else. "Dosage held. Good work, all of you." She looked back at Xander, something almost proud crossing her weathered face. "Your friend's numbers were solid. Damn near perfect, actually."

"I'm gonna cry," Willow said, arriving at a dead run from the treeline where she'd been stationed with the radio, skidding to a stop at the sight of it, torn between terror and pure, undiluted scientific joy. "I'm gonna cry, and it's gonna be happy crying, and nobody's allowed to make fun of me for it."

Alan's team, thoroughly briefed this time and equipped with a transport rig that had taken three weeks and a small fortune to properly assemble, arrived within the hour. The zoo's newest, most heavily secured enclosure broke ground the following week.

"Especially the sabertooth," Willow said, catching Xander's eye over the sedated animal, equal parts exhausted and radiant. "Told you it'd be worth doing right."

"Never doubted it," Xander said, which wasn't completely true, but felt like exactly the right thing to say anyway.

✳

100.55. The Ancient One: What Reaches Backward

The Ancient One felt it while meditating in the high chamber of Kamar-Taj, years before the boy from New York would ever darken her doorstep, decades before the name Strange would mean anything to her at all — a disturbance in the deep structure of things, faint but unmistakable, like a single thread pulled loose from a tapestry woven long before any hand now living had touched it.

She had spent centuries learning to distinguish true disturbances from the ordinary background noise of a living, breathing multiverse — the small ripples of everyday magic, the constant low hum of countless realities going about their business, indifferent to her attention. This was different. This had weight to it, gravity, the quality of something that mattered regardless of how far off it might still be.

She did not speak of it immediately. Some disturbances announced themselves as urgent and proved shallow. Others arrived quiet and proved, on closer study, to run notably deeper than their first impression suggested. This one, she suspected within the hour, belonged to the second category.

She held it for three days, meditation stretching into something closer to vigil, before finally drawing on the Eye, tracing the disturbance as far as her considerable skill allowed, and found herself troubled by what she could, and could not, perceive.

The ripple did not originate in her own time. That much was clear immediately — it arrived already old, already rippling outward from some point she could not yet locate, forward and backward through the years in equal measure, the way a stone dropped in still water sends its rings in every direction rather than merely the direction it happened to fall. She traced it as far back as her skill allowed, and further forward too, and found, at both ends, only the same frustrating absence of a clear source — a wound in time's own fabric, felt everywhere at once and yet visible nowhere in particular.

'Something, someday, will disturb time itself,' she thought, turning the sensation over with the patient care she brought to all genuinely difficult questions. 'Not yet. Not for some while yet, by the shape of what I can perceive. But it is coming, and its disturbance reaches backward far enough to touch even this moment, sitting here, long before whatever causes it has occurred.'

Wong found her still sitting there on the fourth morning, having noticed her absence from the usual training sessions. He'd learned to take her silences seriously.

"Something's been keeping you up here," he said, not quite a question.

"I have."

"Should I be concerned?"

She considered the question with real care before answering, the way she considered most things Wong asked her, having long since learned he rarely asked without reason. "Not yet. Perhaps not for a great while. But eventually, yes. Something is coming that none of us currently living will be equipped to fully understand when it arrives."

"That is not especially comforting."

"No," she agreed. "It is not."

She considered warning someone properly, consulting the other Masters at length, or leaving some record for whoever held her position when the disturbance finally, properly arrived. She decided, in the end, against wider disclosure — some knowledge, held too early, did more harm than the ignorance it replaced, and she had long since learned to trust her instinct on which category any given foresight belonged to.

She did permit herself one small indulgence: a private note, sealed and warded, tucked into the deepest archive of Kamar-Taj, addressed to no one in specific, meant only to be found if and when the disturbance ever properly arrived and someone, someday, thought to look.

Something reaches backward through the years, she wrote, the words coming slowly, each one weighed carefully before committing to the page. I cannot yet see its source, only its shape. When it comes, know that it was felt long before it happened, by someone who chose not to interfere with what had not yet occurred. Whether that was wisdom or cowardice, I leave for whoever reads this to judge for themselves.

She sealed it, set it aside, and returned to her meditation, the disturbance fading back into the deep, quiet static of a timeline that had not yet learned it would, eventually, be broken.

Years passed. The disturbance never fully left her awareness, a faint, persistent hum beneath everything else she attended to — present during her training sessions, present during her rare, careful interventions in the affairs of mortals, present in quiet moments between students when her mind wandered toward questions she'd long since stopped expecting to answer in her own lifetime. It surfaced most clearly late at night, in the hours she reserved for her own private study, a reminder that some problems simply outlasted the people who first noticed them.

Right up until the day a young surgeon with broken hands finally found his way to her door, and her attention turned, as it always eventually did, toward more immediate concerns — a life needing shaping, a mind needing opening, the endless, patient work of preparing someone for a role they didn't yet know they'd eventually have to fill.

She died, in time, the way all things eventually did, never learning the disturbance's true shape, never knowing that an Asgardian hammer would tumble sideways into a small, ordinary town on an Earth not quite her own, carried there by the very fracture she'd felt coming decades before anyone thought to look for it, or that a girl half a world away would one day try and fail to lift that same hammer, only to succeed months later, having grown into whatever the hammer had been waiting for all along.

Somewhere, in whatever came after, she might have found some quiet satisfaction in being right. Or perhaps only in having chosen, all those years ago, not to interfere with something that was never truly hers to prevent — a small, private act of restraint that would, decades later, prove to be exactly the gift a stranger's world had needed most.

✳

100.6. Thor: The Search

Thor had visited eleven realms before this one, and none of them had been Sunnydale, which he was beginning to suspect was less a coincidence and more a joke the universe was playing at his expense.

The first had been a world of endless ice, populated by nothing but silence and wind, cold enough that even he had felt it through layers of enchanted armor, the horizon lost somewhere behind curtains of blowing snow that never seemed to fully clear. He'd searched three days before admitting defeat, the hammer's presence nowhere in evidence, nothing but white in every direction he turned.

The second had been ocean, horizon to horizon, no land he could find no matter how far he flew, waves the size of mountains rolling beneath him in patterns that suggested storms of a scale his own realm had never quite produced. He'd nearly lost himself searching that one, exhausted past the point of good judgment before finally admitting the hammer couldn't possibly have landed somewhere with no ground to land on.

The third had held people — actual people, curious and frightened in equal measure, gathering in the streets of a low, sprawling city built from materials he didn't recognize, watching him with expressions that shifted rapidly from wonder to something closer to alarm. He'd felt nothing of the hammer there, and left quickly, mindful of Heimdall's warnings about disturbing realities unprepared for a god's arrival, the memory of those frightened, upturned faces staying with him longer than he'd expected.

The fourth had been jungle so dense he'd had to cut his own path through it, vines thick as his own arm, insects that bit through even his boots. The fifth had been desert, endless and empty, heat shimmer distorting the horizon into something that looked, more than once, deceptively like the hammer's silhouette before resolving into nothing but rock. The sixth through tenth blurred together in memory, a procession of worlds either empty or wrong or simply, maddeningly silent where the hammer's presence should have been.

The eleventh had nearly broken something in him — a realm caught mid-catastrophe, some disaster he never fully understood the shape of, survivors fleeing something Thor couldn't identify and didn't have time to properly investigate before the absence of any hammer-signal sent him onward again, guilt trailing behind him at having left people in genuine need to continue his own search.

"You are certain it went somewhere findable," he'd said, before setting out, standing on the Bifrost's edge, impatient the way he always was when action was called for and waiting was all that was on offer.

"I am certain of very little where the hammer's own will is concerned," Heimdall had replied, infuriatingly calm as always. "I sent it away for its own protection, during the incursion. Where it chose to land afterward was never entirely mine to control."

"You made it sound simpler than this at the time."

"I made it sound urgent, because it was urgent. I never claimed it would be simple to retrieve." Heimdall's golden eyes had held something almost like amusement. "The hammer seeks worthiness, Thor. It does not seek convenience. You, of all people, should understand the distinction."

Thor had understood the distinction considerably less than he'd claimed to, standing here now on his twelfth attempt, in a realm that looked — astonishingly, after eleven failures — almost totally ordinary. Small structures. Modest transportation devices moving along paved paths. People who looked at him with confusion rather than terror, which he'd come to recognize, after considerable trial, as a promising sign rather than a discouraging one.

He'd felt something here immediately, the faintest thread of the hammer's presence, more concrete than any of the previous eleven attempts had offered. Close. Closer than close, if his instincts held, a resonance he hadn't dared fully trust after so many false starts elsewhere.

'Small world,' he thought, taking in the modest scale of the street around him, the buildings no more than a few stories tall, everything built to a scale that felt, after Asgard's soaring architecture, almost deliberately humble. 'Small, but not without its own strangeness. I sense something beneath the surface here. Something significantly older than the buildings suggest.'

He stood there a long moment simply breathing it in, this ordinary street with its ordinary buildings, letting himself, cautiously, begin to hope this search might actually be nearing its end after weeks that had felt, more than once, endless.

He hadn't expected to find a guide so quickly — the young man who'd stopped dead in the street, taken one long look at him, and immediately, correctly, guessed exactly who he was. That, more than anything, told Thor something worth noting about this realm: whatever strangeness lived beneath its ordinary surface, its people were evidently not strangers to strangeness themselves.

'Perhaps this is finally the place,' he allowed himself to think, following his new acquaintance toward whatever destination the young man had in mind, hope sitting cautious and unfamiliar in his chest after eleven realms of disappointment. 'Perhaps the search ends here, at last, in a small, beige town that has clearly seen far stranger things than one displaced god looking for his hammer.'

He hoped so, at least. Twelve realms was, by any reasonable measure, quite enough searching for one lifetime, even a lifetime as long as his — and some quiet, tired part of him, longer buried than he cared to admit, simply wanted to go home, hammer in hand, and rest.

✳

100.65. Thor: The Ping

Thor felt it the moment it happened — a plucked string resonating somewhere behind his sternum, sharp and sudden against the faint, directionless static he'd carried since the day Heimdall sent the hammer away, a background hum he'd grown so accustomed to that its sudden sharpening nearly staggered him mid-stride through the palace's outer hall.

Contact. Someone had touched it. Properly touched it, gripped its weight, engaged with it as an object meant to be lifted.

And it had answered.

The resonance carried a texture he recognized immediately, distant cousin to the feeling he'd known his entire life holding the hammer himself — not identical, nothing was ever quite identical, but close enough in shape that his breath caught before he'd fully processed why. He stopped where he stood, one hand braced against the nearest wall, drawing the sensation in properly, memorizing its exact shape the way a man memorizes a voice he's afraid he might not hear again.

Worthy. Or worthy enough, in whatever measure the hammer used to judge such things, which Thor had never fully understood even after a lifetime of being its chosen bearer.

He nearly called for the Bifrost that same hour, already turning toward the observatory before some older, more dutiful instinct caught him by the collar and held him in place.

He didn't call for it, because Asgard, in the aftermath of the incursion that had forced the hammer's departure in the first place, was still very much a realm requiring its prince's attention. Border skirmishes flared where old wards had thinned, small conflicts erupting along territories that had gone peacefully undisputed for centuries before the incursion's disruption unsettled old boundaries all at once. Three separate delegations demanded audience over territory the incursion had reshaped, each convinced their claim held precedence, each requiring hours of careful diplomacy Thor had never much enjoyed but had learned, over the years, to perform competently regardless. And beneath all of it, quieter than the skirmishes, quieter than the delegations, his father's health sat as a slow, persistent worry nobody spoke of directly but everybody privately measured against the calendar — a decline gradual enough to deny easily, present enough that Thor found himself, more than once, lingering in doorways just to watch Odin sleep, reassuring himself the breathing continued steady.

A god, it turned out, could feel an unmistakable signal and still not be free to chase it, not for months, not while an entire realm needed steadying first. He told himself this daily, some days believing it more than others.

He carried the resonance with him regardless, turning it over in idle moments between council sessions, holding onto the shape of it like a promise he intended to keep the moment circumstances finally allowed. He confided in no one, not even Heimdall, not wanting to voice hope he wasn't yet free to act on, afraid saying it aloud might somehow make the waiting harder to bear.

Circumstances allowed, eventually, though far later than he'd have liked. The border skirmishes settled, slowly, into new treaties both sides could live with. The delegations concluded their business, satisfied or at least resigned. His father's health, for the moment, held steady enough that Thor could leave without the guilt of abandoning a dying man's bedside. By the time Asgard's affairs settled enough for him to be spared, and by the time Midgard's own troubles pulled him briefly elsewhere besides — a skirmish here, a rescue there, the ordinary obligations of an Avenger that never quite paused simply because his own heart was elsewhere — the trail had gone cold enough that he could no longer simply follow it. The resonance's direction, sharp and immediate in the moment it first arrived, had faded back into something closer to general certainty than precise location, months of delay eroding what should have been a clean, findable thread into something much more diffuse.

Which meant a search. A real one, realm by disconnected realm, since travel between branches unconnected by any established path was never so simple as merely willing himself toward a destination — each attempt required searching blind, feeling for any answering trace of the hammer's presence, then moving on when none came, the process slow and uncertain in a way his usual, more direct approach to problems had never quite prepared him for.

He began in earnest once free, finally, properly free of every obligation that had held him back, and found nothing for longer than he'd have liked to admit, the gap between hope and disappointment widening with every failed realm, patience wearing thinner than he cared to acknowledge even to himself.

✳

100.7. The Compound

Natasha noticed first, which surprised exactly no one, given she generally noticed things before anyone else bothered to look.

She'd started keeping a loose mental log of Thor's comings and goings months back, mostly out of habit rather than any conscious decision to monitor him — the same instinct that kept her tracking exit routes in restaurants and counting heads in crowded rooms, applied without much thought to the people she actually trusted. Which was, she'd come to realize, a fairly short list, and Thor sat near the top of it despite three years of insisting to herself that she didn't really do that anymore, the trusting thing.

"Thor's been gone eleven days," she said, sliding into the briefing room where Steve was reviewing reports nobody particularly wanted to be reviewing, the fluorescent light overhead doing nothing kind for either of their moods.

Steve didn't look up right away, still working through a paragraph on supply logistics that had apparently earned his full concentration. "He does that. Space stuff. Family stuff. Sometimes both at once."

"He usually tells somebody. Even if it's just Bruce, muttering something about cosmic errands on his way out the door." She set a tablet down on the table between them, a single line of text glowing on the screen — a timestamp, a location ping that had gone dark, nothing especially alarming on its own. "He didn't tell anybody this time. Just gone."

Steve frowned at that, setting his own folder aside with the careful deliberateness of someone who took interruptions seriously precisely because he didn't get many of them without good reason. "You think something's wrong?"

"I think Thor doesn't usually vanish quietly. Loud entrances, loud exits, that's kind of his whole brand." Natasha crossed her arms, leaning against the table's edge. "Eleven days of quiet doesn't fit the pattern. Doesn't mean something's definitely wrong. Just means I'm paying attention to it."

"Could be nothing. Could be exactly the kind of Asgard business he doesn't think we need looped in on. He's mentioned before, some of what he deals with back home isn't really ours to know."

"Could be." She didn't sound convinced, tapping one finger against the tablet's edge, a habit that had never quite left her from years of waiting on intelligence that wouldn't confirm itself fast enough. "I put out feelers anyway. Contacted a few people who might've heard something, checked whatever satellite data actually tracks something his size moving through atmosphere. Nothing yet. If he's not back inside a week, I think we actually look into it properly, instead of just noting it and moving on."

Bruce wandered in partway through, coffee in hand, catching the tail end of the conversation with the mild, half-present attention of a man who'd been up too many nights running numbers that hadn't cooperated, hair doing something that suggested he'd forgotten it existed sometime around 3 a.m.

"Thor missing?" he asked, settling into the chair across from Steve without quite seeming to register he was joining a conversation already in progress.

"Thor unaccounted for," Natasha corrected. "Slight difference. He's a grown god. He's allowed to go quiet occasionally."

"He usually tells me before he does, though," Bruce said, echoing Steve's earlier point without quite meaning to, rubbing at his eyes. "We've got a standing science thing. Wednesdays, mostly, sometimes he brings actual Asgardian text he wants translated, sometimes we just argue about whether magic and physics are secretly the same discipline wearing different coats. He missed it. Didn't even send word, which — that's actually pretty unlike him, now that I'm properly thinking about it instead of just being annoyed I lost an afternoon."

The room went quiet for a moment, three people independently arriving at the same low-grade unease without quite wanting to name it out loud first, the kind of silence that settled over people who'd each individually decided, without discussing it, that a pattern was worth taking seriously.

"A week," Steve said finally, decisive in the way he always got once he'd actually made up his mind about something. "If he's not back or in contact within a week, we start actually looking. Properly. Not just noting it and hoping it resolves itself."
