Chapter 128: Chapter 128

"So wait, an actual government agency knows about all this now?" Jesse asked, at lunch, visibly working through the implications with more seriousness than his usual lunch-table energy.

"Apparently," Xander said. "Natasha's boss wants a full report. Giles is working on it with her right now."

"Is that bad? That feels like it could be bad."

"Honestly not sure yet. Could go either way."

Cordelia, who'd been listening with unusual focus, finally spoke up. "Okay, but does this mean we're all, like, government business now? Because I did not sign up to have a file somewhere."

"You probably already have a file somewhere, Cordelia," Willow said. "Given everything that's happened in this town over the years."

"That's deeply unsettling information and I need you to take it back."

"Can't take back a probably-true fact."

"You can try."

Faith, arriving mid-conversation, dropped into her usual seat. "Still on the Avenger thing? Feels like we've talked about nothing else all week."

"It's a genuinely big development," Willow said. "I feel like it deserves the conversation time."

"Fair. Just feels weird, our lunch table being where world-altering information gets casually discussed."

"Welcome to Sunnydale," Xander said. "We've had worse lunch table topics."

"That's true, actually," Jesse admitted. "Remember the werewolf thing?"

"Please don't remind me of the werewolf thing."

"I'm just saying, at least this time nobody's currently trying to eat anyone."

"Genuinely valid point," Faith said. "Low bar, but valid."

The table settled into its usual noise after that, the government-agency revelation slowly giving way to more ordinary concerns — the sandwich debate resurfacing briefly, and Cordelia rounding on Jesse with visible relish.

"So are you ever going to give up, or is this a lifelong project," she said. "I'm asking for planning purposes."

"Give up on what?"

"Don't play dumb, it doesn't suit you. Well, it suits you, but not in a way that's working right now."

"I have no idea what you're referring to."

"You've been not-so-subtly trying to get my attention since the ninth grade, Jesse. I'm simply asking if there's an end date, or if I should just budget for this as a permanent background feature of my life."

"I would never. That's a completely baseless accusation."

"Your face is doing the thing it does when I catch you."

"My face does not have a thing."

"Your face has several things, and I've catalogued all of them."

The whole comfortable chaos of a lunch table that had, by now, absorbed enough impossible things to treat even the newest one with something close to routine acceptance.

"I think it's gonna be okay," Willow said, quieter, mostly to Xander, leaning her head against his shoulder. "Giles is careful. Natasha seems careful too. Feels like the report's in good hands."

"Yeah. I think so too, honestly. Just weird, having it be this big now." He pressed a kiss to the top of her head, the gesture as automatic as breathing by now.

Faith caught the moment from across the table, same as she had a handful of times over the past couple weeks now, and found herself, not for the first time lately, quietly cataloguing exactly how easy it looked, wondering, without quite letting the thought finish itself, what it might feel like to be part of that easy instead of just watching it from a few seats over.

"Big's allowed sometimes. Doesn't mean it's bad."

"No," Xander agreed. "Guess it doesn't."

✳

Chapter 129: Chapter 129

Faith and Buffy both came this time, the three of them landing in a world of high alpine meadows, snow-capped peaks ringing the horizon in every direction under a sky so clear it looked unreal.

"Okay, this one's stunning," Faith said, turning slowly to take it all in. "Genuinely stunning. Different kind of stunning than the lake trip."

"Every trip's got its own kind, apparently," Buffy said. "I've stopped being surprised by how many kinds there are."

They spent the first day working through the meadow, cataloguing wildflowers in colors bright enough to rival anything from previous trips, and found the actual wildlife on the second morning — a herd of long-haired, goat-like creatures grazing at the meadow's edge, utterly unbothered as all three of them walked right up into the middle of the herd without a single animal so much as looking up from the grass.

"They're kind of majestic," Faith said, really charmed, reaching out to run a hand through one's shaggy coat while it kept right on grazing. "Didn't expect majestic. Expected weird, mostly."

"Most of it's weird and majestic at the same time, honestly," Xander said. "That's kind of the whole theme." He glanced at the nearest kid, small and curious, nosing at his sleeve without a trace of the herd's usual wariness. "Also, hey, baby one's friendly. Alan's gonna cry actual tears over this."

They spent the rest of the trip in easy, appreciative quiet, and by the third evening had fell into the kind of comfortable rhythm that came only from real trust between people who'd been through notably more together than any of them had expected when this whole strange life started, one new addition to the zoo's collection curled up contentedly in the makeshift carrier Willow had insisted they start bringing along for exactly this kind of trip.

"This is a good one," Buffy said, watching the last light fade off the peaks. "After everything with Fury, with the report, with all of it — this is exactly the reset I needed."

"Same," Faith agreed. "Feels good, having this be normal now. Having somewhere to just be quiet together."

"Yeah," Xander said, meaning it fully. "Feels like the actual point of all this, honestly. Not just the animals. This part too."

They made it home with goat photos, wildflower samples, and a fresh reminder for all three of them why this strange, complicated life was worth every bit of trouble that came bundled with it.

Willow was waiting up, curled in the doorway with a blanket around her shoulders. "Well?"

"Best one in a while," Buffy confirmed. "Mountains, goats, genuine peace. Ten out of ten."

"I'm so glad," Willow said, and pulled them both in before either had even finished setting their bags down.

✳

Buffy caught up with Faith on patrol that same week, the two of them falling into step together along the cemetery's back row, comfortable in the kind of quiet that came from years of trusting someone at your back.

"Can I say something," Buffy said, eventually, "and you tell me if I'm wrong, and we never bring it up again if I am?"

Faith's steps didn't change, but something in her shoulders went carefully still. "Sure."

"That lake trip. You watched me and Xander for a second longer than you needed to, and it wasn't jealous. It was something else." Buffy kept her eyes forward, giving Faith room not to have to look at her while she answered. "I'm not saying that to put you on the spot. I'm saying it because if I'm right, I don't want you sitting on it alone, wondering if it's even safe to say out loud. It is. Whatever you end up deciding to do with it."

Faith was quiet a long moment, a stake turning over slowly in her hand. "You're not wrong."

"Okay."

"That's it? Just 'okay'?"

"For now, yeah. You don't owe me the rest of it tonight. I just wanted you to know I noticed, and that noticing isn't the same thing as it being weird." Buffy bumped her shoulder against Faith's, easy, familiar. "Take whatever time you need. We're not going anywhere."

Faith let out a breath she hadn't quite realized she'd been holding. "Yeah. Okay. Might take me a minute."

"Take your minute. I've got nowhere else to be."

They finished the patrol in comfortable quiet after that, nothing else needing saying just yet, but something had shifted regardless — a door left open rather than closed, waiting for whenever Faith was actually ready to walk through it.

✳

129.5. Chapter 129.5

Faith waited until the gear was unpacked and the evening had settled into its ordinary quiet before she said anything, which in itself told Xander something was coming — Faith rarely waited for a good moment when a blunt one would do just as well, and the fact that she'd clearly picked this one meant she'd been carrying it a while.

"So," she said, dropping onto the couch across from where the three of them had settled, "I've been thinking about something for a few weeks now, and I'd rather just say it than keep circling it."

"Okay," Buffy said, carefully neutral, the way she got when she could tell something mattered before knowing what it was.

"You three. This thing you've got." Faith gestured vaguely between them, not unkindly. "I've been watching it for a while now. Longer than you probably realized. The way you are with each other. It's not subtle, for the record, in case anybody was under the impression it was subtle."

"We know it's not subtle," Willow said, a little wary.

"I'm not bringing it up to be weird about it. I'm bringing it up because I've been sitting with something since that lake trip, and every week since it's just gotten harder to keep sitting on it instead of saying it out loud." Faith held Xander's eyes for a second, then Buffy's, then Willow's, steady despite the vulnerability underneath it. "I want in. Not — I'm not saying that to be dramatic, or because I think I'm owed anything. I'm saying it because I've thought about it a long time now, and I'd rather be honest and get told no than keep sitting on it and wondering."

The room went quiet for a moment, nobody rushing to fill it.

"Can I ask why now?" Xander said, gently, not a rejection, just genuine curiosity.

"Honestly? The hammer thing helped. Figuring out I was worth something on my own terms, not just compared to somebody else." Faith shrugged, though the gesture cost her more than she let show. "And that lake trip, weeks back. Watching you three just be easy with each other, the way you are, and realizing I wanted that. Not instead of anything. Just — also. If that's something you'd even want. I've caught myself watching it happen more times than I probably should've since then, honestly."

"I noticed you noticing," Buffy admitted. "Wasn't sure what it meant, exactly. Didn't want to assume."

"I've thought about it too," Willow said, surprising herself a little with how easily it came out. "Not, like, actively planning anything. Just — noticed. That you fit, somehow. In a way that made sense before I ever tried to name it."

"Same," Buffy said, quiet. "I didn't want to say anything, because I didn't want to make it weird if it was just me reading into something."

"It wasn't just you," Xander said, something easing in his own chest at finally admitting it out loud. "I've thought about it too. Didn't know how to bring it up, though, given everything else you've had going on lately."

Faith let out a breath she'd clearly been holding since she sat down. "Okay. That's — truly more of a yes than I let myself expect, walking in here."

"It's not a yes to anything specific yet," Buffy said, careful but warm. "It's a yes to actually talking about it properly. Figuring out what it'd even look like, together, instead of just deciding it in one conversation on a couch."

"I can work with that. I wasn't expecting one conversation to solve it anyway." Faith managed a real smile, some of the tension finally leaving her shoulders. "Just needed to say it. Sitting on it for weeks was getting old."

"For what it's worth," Willow said, "I'm glad you said it. All of it. The wanting-in part and the honest-about-why part both."

"Yeah," Xander agreed. "Me too."

They talked well into the night after that — careful, unhurried, nobody rushing toward an answer bigger than the conversation could actually hold in one sitting, and by the time Faith finally headed home, something had shifted in the room that none of them fully had words for yet, but all of them recognized as real.

✳

Chapter 130: Chapter 130

Xander went alone this time, everyone else tied up finishing the report for Fury, and found the world thick with bioluminescent fungi covering every fallen log and shaded hollow, glowing faint blue-green even in daylight.

"Okay," he said, crouching to get a closer look. "This is honestly beautiful. Didn't expect glowing mushrooms today."

He spent the first day documenting the fungi, and found the actual wildlife after dark on the second night — small, moth-like creatures drawn to the glow, wings patterned to mimic the fungal light so closely he nearly missed them entirely against the forest floor.

"That's some genuinely impressive camouflage," he told them, getting his photos carefully by the dim, natural light. "Willow's gonna have a field day with this one."

He spent the rest of the trip documenting the strange, glowing ecosystem, and by the time the jump pulled him home, he had photos unlike anything he'd brought back before, and a fresh reminder of why solitary jumps still mattered even now that company had become the norm.

Willow caught him at the door, papers from the Fury report still scattered across the kitchen table behind her, and kissed him hello before either of them said another word. "Okay. Now tell me."

"Really good one. Glowing mushrooms, camouflaged moths. Genuinely one of the coolest ones yet." He glanced past her toward the table. "Faith still buried in this too?"

"She left an hour ago. Said she needed air. I think the whole 'talking through what this actually looks like' conversation is still settling for her, same as it is for the rest of us."

"Yeah. Same, honestly. Good settling, though. Just a lot to hold at once."

"I need every photo. Immediately." Willow pushed the report papers aside, visibly grateful for the distraction. "How was it, being out there alone again? After all the group trips lately?"

Xander considered the question seriously. "Good, actually. Different good. Reminded me it's still my thing too, even with everyone else along most of the time now."

"That makes sense. Glad you got that reminder."

"Yeah. Me too." He handed over the camera, already reaching for a chair. "How's the report coming?"

"Slowly. Giles keeps rewriting sections. Wants it exactly right before it goes anywhere."

"Good instinct, probably, given who's reading it."

"That's what I keep telling him," Willow said, already scrolling through the glowing mushroom photos with visible delight, momentarily entirely absorbed by something that had nothing to do with Fury or reports or any of the rest of it.

✳

Xander brought Davy the finished report on his next trip through, same as he'd promised Cent he would, spreading the pages across the kitchen table while Millie poured coffee nobody had asked for and Davy read in careful, methodical silence, the same unhurried thoroughness he brought to preflight checklists and grading decisions alike.

He read it twice before saying anything.

"This is good," he said finally, setting the last page down. "Careful. Doesn't overstate what you don't actually know, doesn't understate what you do. I've read worse reports from people with a lot more training than whoever wrote this."

"Giles, mostly. With Natasha's editing."

"Tell them both they did right by you." Davy tapped the page laying out the fracture theory, thoughtful. "One thing. This part, about where the ability actually comes from — you didn't mention us at all. Our side of it."

"Didn't think that was mine to include, without asking first."

Davy considered that a long moment, something like real respect settling into his expression. "Good instinct. Appreciate you thinking of it, actually asking instead of just deciding for us." He slid the pages back across the table. "Send it as it is. And Xander — thank you for bringing it. You didn't have to. Means something that you did."

"Cent said you'd want to see it. Wasn't gonna not do that."

"She's got good instincts too. Wonder where she gets that." Davy's mouth twitched, almost a smile. "Go on. Send your report. We'll be right here regardless of what comes back."

Chapter 131: Chapter 131

Fury's response came back faster than anyone expected, and considerably more measured than Xander had braced for, given everything he'd heard about the man's reputation secondhand — the eyepatch, the sense that he'd seen too much to be surprised by anything, the way even Natasha occasionally chose her words a little more carefully whenever his name came up in conversation.

They gathered in the Magic Box's back room to hear it, the same table that had hosted every major planning session of the past several months now bearing the weight of one more, Giles pouring tea nobody had asked for out of the same nervous habit he always fell into when a conversation mattered more than he wanted to admit out loud.

"He wants a meeting," Natasha said, reading from her tablet, having stayed an extra day specifically to deliver whatever answer came back rather than relay it over an insecure line. "Not an extraction. Not a lockdown. A meeting. Small group, controlled setting, his choice of location."

"That's... actually better than I expected," Giles admitted, setting the teapot down with visible relief.

"He's not reckless. Whatever reputation's reached you, the actual man's more careful than that." Natasha set the tablet down, screen still glowing with a message Xander could see but not quite parse from his angle. "He wants Thor there. Wants me there. Says he'd like you there too, Giles, given your research. And Xander, since it's his ability at the center of all this."

"He wants me to fly to wherever this meeting happens?" Xander asked, already running through logistics in his head — school, the jump schedule, whether Joyce would need advance warning about hosting a federal agency inside her own living room. "Because that seems like a logistics problem, given everything else going on."

"He's coming here, actually. Says Sunnydale's already proven it can keep a secret this size. Doesn't see a reason to relocate the conversation."

Giles removed his glasses, cleaning them slowly — the tell Xander had learned to read as genuine uncertainty rather than mere thoughtfulness. "That's either remarkably reasonable or remarkably concerning, depending on how one chooses to read it."

"Probably both," Natasha said. "That's usually how it goes with him. He doesn't do anything without a reason, and the reasons aren't always the ones you'd guess at first."

Thor, who'd been quiet through most of this, weighing the conversation carefully before contributing to it, finally spoke. "I trust your assessment of him, Natasha. If you believe this is a genuine attempt at understanding rather than something more forceful, I am inclined to believe it too."

"I do believe that. He's not perfect, but he takes real threats seriously and real discoveries seriously. This qualifies as both, from everything the report actually laid out."

"What did you end up adding, at the end? The note." Xander asked, a small, anxious edge creeping into the question despite himself. "Never actually saw that part. Wasn't sure it was mine to read before you sent it."

"Enough to make him take it seriously. Not enough to make him panic. There's a balance to that kind of writing." Natasha's expression softened slightly, reading the anxiety underneath his question. "I put my own name behind Giles's assessment, plainly, in my own words. Said I'd vouch for it personally — that you're exactly what the report says you are, a kid who stumbled into something enormous and has spent four years handling it better than most trained professionals would have. Giles did the real work, three weeks of rewrites and all. I just made sure the ending carried enough weight that Fury couldn't read it and walk away unconvinced."

That landed somewhere Xander hadn't quite expected it to, and he found himself quiet a moment before responding. "Thanks. Genuinely. Both of you."

"It's the truth. Giles doesn't waste good writing on flattery, and neither do I."

Xander needed a long moment to take in the size of it, turning the whole conversation over. "So this is really happening. An actual government official, crossing over from an entirely different reality, coming here, to talk about all this directly. In my town. Probably in this actual room, knowing how things have been going lately."

"It's really happening," Natasha confirmed. "Next week, most likely. I'll stay to help coordinate the logistics — security, timing, making sure nothing catches anyone off guard on either side."

"That's... a lot."

"It is. You've handled a lot already, though. I think you'll handle this too."

Xander appreciated the vote of confidence more than he let on, and the room slipped into a thoughtful quiet, everyone processing the size of what was coming, Thor's hand finding Xander's shoulder briefly, steadying, the same easy warmth he seemed to bring to most things even in moments carrying real weight.

"Whatever happens," Thor said, "you will not face it alone. That much I can promise you."

"Yeah," Xander said, feeling something settle in his chest that had been unsteady since the conversation started. "Yeah, I'm starting to actually believe that part."

Giles, still holding his glasses, finally put them back on, something resolved in his expression. "Then I suppose we have a week to prepare properly, rather than simply react to whatever arrives. I'd rather we used it well."

"Agreed," Natasha said, already pulling up a fresh document on her tablet, planning already underway before anyone else in the room had fully finished processing the news at all.

✳

Chapter 132: Chapter 132

Buffy and Faith both came this time, all three of them wanting something ordinary before whatever Fury's visit turned out to bring the following week.

The world was prairie, endless grass rolling toward a horizon broken only by distant, scattered trees, wind carrying the smell of earth and growing things.

"Okay, this one's simple," Faith said, taking it in. "Simple's good right about now."

"Simple's great right about now," Buffy agreed. "I could use a few days of nothing complicated."

They spent the first two days working the grassland, cataloguing a herd of long-horned creatures that grazed without much concern for their presence, and found a den of small, burrowing animals on the third morning, popping up and vanishing at the sound of footsteps with a speed that had all three of them laughing within minutes.

"They're so fast," Faith said, delighted despite herself. "Didn't expect to be charmed by burrowing rodents today, but here we are."

"Life's full of surprises," Xander said. "That's basically the whole job description."

They spent the rest of the trip in easy, appreciative quiet, and by the time the jump pulled them home, all three of them felt lighter, steadier, better prepared for whatever the coming week actually held.

Faith caught Xander's hand at one point, the two of them just walking, tentative about it in a way that didn't match her usual easy confidence, glancing at Buffy like she was still checking whether this was actually allowed.

"You can, you know," Buffy said, catching the look, taking Faith's other hand herself without making a thing of it. "We talked about it. This is allowed."

"Still feels new. Gonna take me a minute to stop double-checking."

"Take all the minutes you need," Xander said, squeezing her hand once. "Nobody's in a hurry here."

Joyce was up marking something in her list when they came in, still holding hands, none of them quite ready to let go of it yet. She looked up, taking in all three of them at once — the held hands included — and didn't comment on it. "Good one?"

"Really good one," Buffy confirmed. "Prairie, funny rodents, zero drama. Exactly what we all needed before next week."

"I'm glad," Joyce said, and went back to her list, leaving them the doorway and whatever they wanted to do with it.

133. Cent: "Fury's coming in person?"

Xander told me the news the next time he made it back to the fence line. I could tell how much he was still processing it himself.

"Fury's coming to Sunnydale. Next week. Wants an actual meeting."

I sat down hard. "In person. Not a call, not a report review. Him. There."

"Yeah. Natasha says it's a good sign, actually. Says he's careful, not reckless."

"That's Natasha's read. Doesn't mean it's the right one."

"Fair. I'm choosing to trust her, though. She knows him better than any of us."

I told Dad that same evening. Watched him go very still — the stillness that means he's already running through every worst version of the story before he lets himself hope for a better one.

"In person," he repeated. "That's not standard protocol for a first contact. That's either genuine respect, or it's leverage-gathering dressed up as respect."

"Xander trusts Natasha's read on it."

"Xander hasn't spent thirty years learning what these people are capable of when they decide something's worth controlling instead of just understanding."

"I know, Dad. I'm not arguing that part. I'm just saying maybe this one's different."

He was quiet a long moment, turning it over. "Maybe. I want more information before deciding how worried to actually be."

"What kind of information?"

"Everything Natasha's willing to share about Fury specifically. His history. How he's handled situations like this before, if there's precedent." He met my eyes. "I'm not saying stop it. I'm saying I want us going in with real information, not just one woman's personal endorsement, however much you trust her."

"That's fair. I'll ask."

Mom, quiet through most of this, finally spoke. "Do you want to be there? For the meeting?"

I hadn't actually thought about it until she asked. "I don't know. Feels like it's Xander's thing to navigate, mostly."

"It's also your thing, Cent. You're the reason any of this connected in the first place."

I turned that over. "Maybe. I'll think about it."

"Take your time," Dad said. "Nothing about this needs deciding tonight."

I went to bed that night turning the whole thing over — Fury, the meeting, Dad's careful, earned caution, my own uncertain place in whatever came next. Big week coming. Bigger than any of us fully understood yet, probably.

Hoped we were ready for it. Wasn't fully sure we were.

✳

133.5. Wong: The Sealed Note

Wong found the note by accident, the way most actually important discoveries at Kamar-Taj tended to surface — while searching the deep archive for something else completely, a minor ward-maintenance record from three centuries back, candlelight throwing long shadows across shelves that stretched further into the dark than any ordinary building's architecture should reasonably have allowed, and finding instead a small, warded envelope tucked behind it, sealed in a hand he recognized immediately even after all this time.

He almost set it aside. The archive held decades of the Ancient One's private notes, most of them mundane, catalogued and studied long ago by scholars far more patient than his current schedule allowed for — inventory lists, minor ward adjustments, the accumulated administrative residue of centuries spent maintaining a sanctum most of the world never knew existed.

Something about this one held his attention anyway. The seal, perhaps — heavier than her usual work, layered with wards that felt, even from a distance, much more deliberate than her typical filing habits suggested.

He broke the seal carefully, the wards dissolving at his touch easily, the way protections meant to open for exactly the right person did, rather than resisting everyone indefinitely, and read the single page twice before setting it down, feeling something cold settle in his chest that had nothing to do with the archive's usual chill.

Something reaches backward through the years, it read, her handwriting steady and precise despite whatever uncertainty the words themselves carried. I cannot yet see its source, only its shape. When it comes, know that it was felt long before it happened. Whether that was wisdom or cowardice, I leave for whoever reads this to judge for themselves.

He checked the note's dating against his own records — decades old, written well before the Ancient One's death, well before anyone had reason to suspect the kind of disturbance she was describing, a full generation of scholars passing through Kamar-Taj without ever once suspecting this particular truth sat waiting in the archive beneath their own feet.

'She felt this coming,' he thought, turning the implication over carefully, the candlelight flickering as some draft found its way through the ancient stonework. 'Whatever this is, she sensed it reaching backward through time itself, years before it actually happened.' It was, he had to admit, exactly the kind of foresight he'd come to expect from her, and exactly the kind of restraint too — choosing silence rather than a warning that might have done more harm than the waiting, a judgment call he found himself, even now, unable to fully agree or disagree with.

He brought it to Strange that same evening, finding him deep in his own research, several books open across the sanctum's main table, the Cloak of Levitation draped over the back of his chair like a patient, watchful presence.

"You should see this," Wong said, setting the note down without preamble.

Strange read it twice, the same way Wong had, his expression shifting from mild curiosity to something notably more focused by the second pass, one hand absently rubbing at the old scarring across his knuckles the way he always did when something demanded his full, uninterrupted attention.

"This is dated years before the Snap. Years before the whole time heist." Strange looked up, something sharpening behind his eyes. "You think this is connected to the instability we've been tracking? The strange readings coming off certain realities?"

"I think it would be a remarkable coincidence if it weren't."

"Yeah. I think so too." Strange set the note down carefully, staring at it like it might yield more if he simply looked hard enough, fingers drumming once against the table's edge. "We've had reports — vague, unconfirmed — of anomalous activity. A concentration of it, actually, around one specific reality. Small town. Unremarkable, by most measures, except for the readings."

"Unremarkable towns rarely produce readings worth this much attention."

"No. They don't." Strange met Wong's eyes. "I think it might be time we actually looked into this properly. Directly, rather than just monitoring from a distance. We've been cautious long enough. If she felt this coming decades ago and chose not to act, maybe the lesson isn't caution. Maybe it's that somebody eventually has to."

"I agree." Wong glanced at the note once more, something almost reverent in the gesture. "She felt this coming, decades before any of us understood what it would actually mean. Feels like the least we can do is finally go see what she was trying to warn us about."

"Agreed." Strange was already reaching for his cloak, which lifted itself expectantly off the chair back before he'd even fully extended his hand, something settling into focused purpose behind his eyes. "Let's go find out what's actually happening in this town of theirs."

"You're certain you don't want to gather more information first? Standard protocol would suggest reconnaissance before direct contact."

"Standard protocol also suggested we had years before this became urgent, and apparently we don't." Strange fastened the cloak's clasp, settling into the calm he always wore once a decision had really been made rather than merely considered. "Sometimes the fastest way to gather information is to just walk in and ask. I've got a feeling this is one of those times."

"Fair enough." Wong folded the letter carefully, tucking it into an inner pocket rather than returning it to the archive. "I'm coming with you. Somebody should keep you from asking questions in ways that get us both banned from a reality before we've even finished the introductions."

"That happened one time."

"It happened three times, and I was there for all three."

Strange didn't argue the point, already tracing the first lines of a portal into the air between them, gold light spilling out to fill the room, and the two of them stepped through together, leaving the archive quiet behind them, one more small mystery finally, properly set into motion after decades of patient, careful waiting.

✳

133.6. Mom: The Reconnection

The controller had sat dormant in its hiding place for years, tucked behind a false panel in the barn that Dad had built specifically for it, back when hiding it had still felt necessary rather than just habit. Long enough that Mom had stopped expecting it to ever do anything again — long enough that finding it, these days, felt more like visiting a grave than checking equipment.

The morning it finally chimed with an incoming signal, she was halfway through her second cup of coffee, the house quiet around her, and her hands shook badly enough that she had to set the mug down before she could even reach for the device sitting in her lap.

Home. After all this time, actually Home.

She'd rehearsed this moment more times than she'd ever admitted to anyone, in the quiet years after Max vanished — turning the words over during long drives, during sleepless nights, during the ordinary domestic silences where her mind still, after everything, occasionally drifted back to a mission she'd told herself was over. None of her rehearsals had prepared her for how it would actually feel, sitting here now with the signal truly live for the first time in over a decade — old grief and old duty both surfacing at once, tangled together too tightly to easily separate.

She carried the controller out to the barn before answering, needing the privacy, needing the same walls that had held this secret for so many years to hold this first conversation too.

"This is Miriam," she said, voice steadier than she expected, though her free hand gripped the workbench edge hard enough to whiten her knuckles. "Formerly assigned to the tame-side observation post. Reporting in, considerably later than protocol allows."

The response took a moment, static clearing into a voice she hadn't heard in decades, careful and measured, an older man's voice she half-recognized despite the years. "Miriam. We'd marked your post as lost, following your brother's disappearance. This is... an unexpected relief."

"The gate's active again. My son found it, quite by accident. I'll explain the full circumstances properly, but I wanted to report in first, given what else I've observed since reconnecting."

"Go ahead."

She told them everything — the passenger pigeons, the contamination scare, the whole strange unraveling that had brought her family, finally, fully into the truth of who she actually was. And then, carefully, the readings she'd only recently started properly understanding: anomalous cross-path activity, far beyond anything the old monitoring protocols had ever accounted for, concentrated heavily around one particular corner of this world.

The silence on the other end stretched long enough that she wondered if the connection had dropped.

"Miriam," the voice said finally, careful in a new way now, "how confident are you in these readings?"

"Very. I've cross-referenced them against the old baseline data three times now. This isn't background noise. Something has changed, significantly, and recently."

Another long pause, longer this time, the kind of silence that carried its own weight even across an imperfect connection. "This matches disturbances we've been tracking independently, on our end. We believed them isolated to our own instruments. If you're seeing corresponding activity from the tame side, that suggests the scope is significantly larger than we'd initially assessed."

"I was afraid you'd say that."

"We need eyes on the ground. Directly. Given your history with the post, and given the family connections you've maintained despite the years of silence, you're the most logical liaison, if you're willing."

Mom closed her eyes, feeling the weight of the offer settle over her — the mission again, after all these years of believing it over, reopening at exactly the moment she'd finally, fully let herself belong to an ordinary life instead. Twenty years of careful distance, undone in the space of a single morning conversation.

"I'm willing," she said, "but I want my family with me. Fully informed. No more secrets, not after everything we've already been through because of the ones I kept before. If this is real, they deserve to know exactly what it costs, going in."

"That can be arranged. We'll send a team to coordinate with you directly. Given the scope of what you're describing, I don't think this is something we can afford to investigate lightly."

"Understood. How soon?"

"As soon as we can manage it. This is not a small concern, Miriam. If the readings are accurate, this may be the most significant anomaly we've encountered since the gate itself was first discovered."

The line went quiet after that, the conversation apparently concluded, and Mom held it a long moment in the barn's familiar dimness, the controller heavy in her hands, the weight of an old mission and a new, unprecedented crisis settling over her all at once.

She found Charlie in the kitchen a while later, still working through the same physics assignment he'd been staring at unproductively for the better part of an hour, and pulled out the chair across from him without preamble.

"I need to tell you something. Something big."

He set his pencil down, reading her face with an attention he'd gotten better at over the past year, ever since the truth about her had first come apart at the seams. "Home's back."

"Home's back," she confirmed. "And there's a problem. A real one. Bigger than anything we dealt with before."

"How big?"

"Big enough they're sending an actual team. Big enough I might need to go with them, at least for a while." She turned the controller over once in her hands. "It's the world where Davy's family is. Where you and Clara found the fence line that first summer."

Charlie went very still at that. "Is Cent's family okay?"

"I don't know yet. That's part of what I need to go find out."

He was quiet a moment, processing, and when he spoke again his voice carried more steadiness than she'd expected, or maybe than she deserved after everything. "You're telling me this time. Instead of just disappearing and letting me figure it out after."

"I'm telling you this time. All of it, as it happens, not after the fact." She reached across the table, covering his hand with hers, the same simple gesture she'd used his whole life for the things that actually mattered too much for words to carry properly. "I promised myself that much, at least, once the truth finally came out. You deserve better than secrets, even necessary ones."

"Even scary ones?"

"Especially scary ones. Those are the ones that matter most to actually share."

He managed something close to a smile at that, tired but real. "Okay. So tell me. All of it. Don't leave anything out this time."

She told him everything, start to finish, watching her son absorb news that would have unsettled most people twice his age, and found herself, not for the first time this past year, deeply, quietly proud of exactly who he'd turned out to be.

✳

133.7. Home: The Decision

The Council met within the hour, which in itself told everyone present how seriously Miriam's report had been received — Home's leadership rarely moved quickly on anything, a habit born of decades spent rebuilding with more caution than speed, careful deliberation baked into every institution they'd rebuilt from a population once small enough to fit inside a single settlement. But this, apparently, warranted an exception, chairs still being pulled into place as Director Osei entered the low, unadorned chamber that served, these days, for meetings requiring genuine urgency.

"Twenty million," Director Osei said, without preamble, once the small group had assembled, the number landing with the weight it always carried in that room, spoken aloud rarely and never lightly. "That's what we came from. I don't need to remind anyone in this room what a single unchecked breach in the boundary between realities can cost, if we're careless about how we approach this."

"Nobody's suggesting carelessness," said Ilsa Kovač, their chief instrumentation officer, the one who'd first flagged the anomalous readings weeks earlier, tablet already open on the table in front of her, data scrolling steadily across its surface. "But we can't simply observe from a distance either. Whatever's happening on the tame side, it's accelerating. Every reading we've taken this month shows increased instability compared to the last."

"Which is precisely why I want to move carefully," Osei said. "We have exactly one confirmed asset with any history on that world, and she's been disconnected from us for over a decade. We don't know her current reliability, her family's current disposition, or what condition the tame side is actually in beyond what her single report describes."

"She sounded credible," said Amara, the youngest member present, still finding her footing in these discussions but unwilling to stay silent on this one, hands folded tight on the table in front of her. "Frightened, but credible. I don't think she'd exaggerate something like this. You could hear it in her voice — she wasn't performing urgency. She was carrying it."

"Fear doesn't guarantee accuracy. It guarantees urgency, which isn't the same thing." Osei considered the room a long moment, gaze moving across each face in turn, the weight of decades of careful leadership visible in the deliberate way she took her time before continuing. "That said, I agree we can't simply wait. Ilsa, you'll lead the team. Small. Two others, no more, given our current resource constraints. I want this handled with the absolute minimum footprint we can manage."

"Understood." Ilsa hesitated, glancing down at her tablet before looking back up. "Miriam offered to serve as liaison directly. Given her family's existing foothold there, it would ease the initial contact far."

"Use her. Carefully. She's earned back some trust, reconnecting on her own initiative rather than waiting to be found, but she's still an unknown quantity after this many years apart from the mission." Osei's expression softened, just slightly, something almost sympathetic crossing her otherwise composed features. "We are not the civilization we once were, before the collapse. We cannot afford to treat this lightly, and we cannot afford to overextend ourselves chasing every anomaly with the same resources we once had to spare. Choose your team well. Move carefully. And Ilsa — if this proves as significant as the readings suggest, do not hesitate to request more support. Pride cost us dearly once already. I won't have it cost us again."

"Understood," Ilsa said again, quieter this time, the weight of the assignment settling properly now that the meeting's urgency had given way to its actual stakes.

Amara spoke up once more, hesitant. "What if it's worse than the readings suggest? What if three people isn't enough?"

"Then Ilsa requests more, and we send more," Osei said, simply. "We are cautious. We are not paralyzed. There is a difference, and I intend for this Council to remember it, whatever comes of this mission."

The room settled at that, something like resolve replacing the earlier tension, and Osei rose, signaling the meeting's end with the same economy she brought to everything.

The Council dispersed shortly after, the decision made, the team already beginning preparations for a journey none of them had ever expected to make — back to a world most of Home's current generation knew only through old records and half-remembered stories, now apparently sitting at the center of something none of them yet fully understood, a small, careful delegation preparing to step, for the first time in over a decade, back into a reality their own history had taught them, above all else, to approach with the deepest possible respect.

✳

Chapter 134: Chapter 134

"Nick Fury," Jesse said, staring at his tray like it might explain the sentence better than Xander had. "The actual director guy. Coming here."

"Apparently," Xander confirmed. "Next week."

"Okay, I need to know if I'm supposed to be scared of this development or excited about it, because I'm currently just confused."

"Little of both, probably," Faith said. "That's kind of the theme of everything lately."

Cordelia, predictably, had already moved past concern into logistics. "Is he bringing security? Because if there's an actual government motorcade in this town, I need to know so I can plan my outfit around potentially being on camera."

"There's not going to be a motorcade, Cordelia."

"You don't know that. Government officials love motorcades."

"I feel like a small-town visit specifically avoids motorcades for exactly the reasons you're describing."

"Fine. But if there IS one, I called it."

Willow, quieter than usual, finally spoke up. "Does anyone else feel like this is the point where the story stops being ours? Like, up until now, it's been our thing — us figuring it out, us deciding what to do about it. Now there's a whole agency involved."

The table went quiet for a moment, the weight of her observation landing harder than the lunchtime chatter around it had prepared anyone for.

"I don't think it stops being ours," Xander said slowly, working through it as he spoke, reaching over to take her hand. "I think it just means more people get to help carry it. Doesn't mean we lose it."

"Maybe," Willow said, holding on. "I hope you're right."

"Me too, honestly."

Faith reached over, bumping Willow's shoulder with her own, a small, grounding gesture that seemed to ease something in her expression. "Whatever happens, we're still the ones who actually understand it best. That's gotta count for something, government agency or not."

"Yeah," Willow said, managing a real smile. "Yeah, I think it does."

The table settled back into its usual noise after that, the government-agency anxiety folding, gradually, back into ordinary lunchtime chaos — the sandwich debate making its inevitable reappearance, Jesse angling for details about Fury that nobody could actually provide, the whole comfortable rhythm of a group that had, by now, gotten remarkably good at holding both the enormous and the ordinary at once, without letting either one crowd out the other.

Chapter 135: Chapter 135

Xander went alone this time, needing a little space to think before the week ahead, and found the world thick with autumn color despite the fact that no season back home matched it — deep reds and golds covering hills that stretched further than he could see.

"Okay," he said, taking it in. "This one's just pretty. No catch yet. I'll take it."

He spent the first day simply walking the hills, cataloguing a flock of birds migrating through in numbers dense enough to darken the sky briefly as they passed overhead, and found a quiet clearing on the second afternoon that felt, for reasons he couldn't quite articulate, like exactly the right place to sit with everything currently weighing on him.

'Fury. Natasha. The report. Cent's dad wanting information before deciding how worried to be.' He turned it all over slowly, methodically, the way he'd learned to over four years of solitary processing. 'Big week coming. Bigger than anything since the ritual, maybe.' It felt good, having the space to actually think it through properly, rather than just reacting to whatever came next.

He spent the rest of the trip in that same quiet, reflective rhythm, and by the time the jump pulled him home, he felt steadier than he had in days, the autumn hills having done exactly the work he'd needed them to.

Buffy kissed him hello before he'd even set his bag down, then pulled back to actually look at him. "You've got that sorted-something-out face."

"Yeah. Good one. Quiet. Needed the quiet, actually, before everything next week."

"You ready for it? Fury, all of it?"

"Getting there. Feels less scary every time I actually sit with it instead of just reacting." He managed a real smile. "Think that's kind of become my whole strategy for handling big scary things lately. Sit with it, then face it."

"Good strategy. Seems to be working."

"Yeah," Xander said, meaning it fully. "Yeah, I think it actually is."

Chapter 136: Chapter 136

"Okay, so let me get this straight," Xander said, staring at the whiteboard Willow had set up in the Magic Box, covered in names and arrows that had multiplied much over the past week, connected by so many colored lines that the whole thing had started to resemble a conspiracy theorist's wall more than a coordination schedule. "Fury's coming. Strange and some guy named Wong are coming. And now apparently a whole team from another reality entirely, coordinated through Cent's family's contact at Home, is also coming."

"That's the current count, yes," Giles confirmed. "All within roughly the same two-week window, from what I understand."

"That's a lot of very powerful, very informed people converging on one small town at once."

"It is," Giles admitted, adjusting his glasses, visibly weary of rapidly running out of shelf space for new, increasingly improbable facts. "I hadn't fully appreciated the scope of this until seeing it laid out quite so plainly."

Willow tapped the board. "The good news is, everyone seems to be approaching this carefully. Nobody's coming in hostile. Everyone wants information, not conflict."

"That's the good news," Faith said, arriving in time to catch the tail end of it, dropping her bag by the door. "What's the bad news?"

"The bad news is our kitchen table's about to become the most contested piece of real estate in several realities at once," Xander said. "I genuinely don't know how we're supposed to host all of this."

"We'll manage," Joyce said, appearing with a fresh pot of coffee like she'd been anticipating exactly this crisis, already mentally cataloguing, from the look on her face, exactly how many extra chairs the dining room could accommodate. "I've hosted apocalypses before. I can host a diplomatic summit."

"This might be bigger than an apocalypse, Mom."

"Everything feels bigger the first time. I've learned not to trust that feeling too much." Joyce set the coffee down, already reaching for a notepad of her own, totally separate from Willow's whiteboards. "We'll need a schedule, probably. Can't have everyone arriving at once. And I'll need to know dietary restrictions. Gods and government officials probably have opinions about food."

"I honestly have no idea if Thor has dietary restrictions."

"I do not," Thor offered helpfully, from across the room. "I eat essentially anything, in essentially any quantity."

"Noted," Joyce said, unbothered, adding a line to her notepad that Xander suspected read something close to unlimited food, prepare accordingly.

"Already working on it," Willow said, gesturing at a second whiteboard nobody had noticed yet, covered in a carefully color-coded timeline, dates and arrival windows mapped out with a precision that suggested she'd been at this for considerably longer than the conversation implied. "Fury first, since his visit was arranged first. Then Strange and Wong, probably a few days after. Home's team last, since they've got the furthest to travel, relatively speaking."

"You made a whole schedule." Xander pressed a kiss to her temple, actually impressed despite himself.

"Of course I made a whole schedule. Somebody had to. Do you know how much worse this would be if two separate reality-crossing delegations showed up on the same afternoon? I don't even want to think about the scheduling conflict."

"I feel like you've already thought about it, though. Extensively."

"I have. It was bad. Very bad. Multiple color-coded worst-case scenarios."

Cordelia, arriving partway through with her own opinions already fully formed, surveyed the whiteboard with visible skepticism. "This feels like it should come with, I don't know, actual security. Are we sure a magic shop is the right venue for this many important people at once? Because if this goes badly, I want it on record that I suggested literally anywhere else."

"We considered the school gym," Giles admitted. "It seemed notably harder to explain to the janitorial staff."

"Fair point."

Thor, who'd been quiet through most of this, finally spoke. "I confess I find this entire situation somewhat validating. Even my own realm rarely manages this many important visitors within so short a span."

"Is that supposed to be reassuring?"

"Only slightly. Mostly I simply find it interesting." Thor considered the whiteboard with genuine curiosity, leaning in close enough to read Willow's smaller annotations. "This town continues to exceed my expectations at every turn."

"Yeah, that tracks with basically everyone's reaction to Sunnydale eventually," Xander said. "Welcome to the club."

"I have been a member of this club for some while now, Xander. I believe my membership is well established at this point."

"Fair. Founding member status, basically."

The group settled into planning after that, dividing tasks, working through logistics that felt, despite the sheer scale of what was actually converging on them, oddly manageable once properly organized — Joyce handling hospitality, Willow handling the schedule, Giles quietly cross-referencing everyone's expected arrival against whatever research still needed finishing beforehand — one more impossible situation folded into a life that had, by now, gotten remarkably skilled at handling exactly this kind of overwhelming complexity without losing its footing utterly.

✳

Chapter 137: Chapter 137

Buffy came along this time, both of them wanting a genuine break before the convergence of visitors currently scheduled to descend on the household over the following weeks.

The world was highland moor, purple heather stretching in every direction under a sky heavy with slow-moving clouds, wind carrying a chill that hinted at snow not far off.

"Okay, this one's moody," Buffy said, pulling her jacket tighter. "Very moody-moor energy."

"Very moody-moor energy that's apparently also home to something interesting," Xander said, spotting movement in the heather. "Look."

Small, hare-like creatures bolted at their approach, quick and skittish in a way that made both of them laugh at how thoroughly they'd startled the local wildlife just by existing.

"Okay, they're fast," Buffy said, watching one vanish into the heather. "Genuinely fast."

"Everything out here seems built for speed. Guess the wind explains that."

They spent the trip working the moor's edges, cataloguing what wildlife they could get close enough to properly document, and by the third evening had eased into the easy, appreciative quiet that came from a trip asking nothing complicated of either of them.

"This is good," Buffy said, watching the last light fade over the heather. "After everything coming up — Fury, the sorcerers, whoever's coming from Cent's family's people at Home — this is exactly the reset I needed."

"Yeah. Same, honestly. Feels like these quiet ones matter even more right now."

"They do." She looked over at him. "You ready for all of it? Everyone converging at once?"

"Getting there. Feels less overwhelming every time I actually sit with it instead of just reacting to the next new arrival."

"Good strategy. Seems to keep working for you."

"Yeah," Xander said, meaning it fully. "Yeah, I think it actually does."

They made it home with hare photos and windburned cheeks, carrying exactly the grounding they needed before the really enormous few weeks ahead.

Willow kissed Buffy hello, then Xander, already tugging them both toward the kitchen. "Come tell me while I heat up food, I haven't eaten since you left."

"Quiet moor, fast hares, zero complications," Buffy said, letting herself be towed along. "Exactly what we needed."

"I'm glad," Willow said, already pulling plates down.

✳

137.5. Ilsa: Preparing to Cross

Ilsa chose her team carefully, weighing capability against the strict resource limits Osei had made clear weren't merely bureaucratic caution but genuine, hard-won necessity, spending two full days reviewing personnel files in the small, cramped office that served as her department's headquarters, coffee going cold beside her more than once as she worked.

She chose Petra first, a field biologist with considerable experience assessing unfamiliar biomes quickly and safely — useful, given how little anyone actually knew about current conditions on the tame side beyond Miriam's brief, urgent report, and useful too for the calm Petra brought to unfamiliar environments, a steadiness Ilsa had personally witnessed twice before on significantly smaller assignments.

Her second choice took longer. She eventually settled on Dov, young for the assignment but sharp, with a gift for languages and cultural assessment that Ilsa suspected would matter more than any of them currently appreciated, given how little Home's current generation actually understood about the tame side's present-day culture — decades of disconnection having left even their most current records badly out of date.

"Three of us," Petra said, reviewing the assignment brief with visible unease, papers spread across the small conference table where the team had gathered for their first formal briefing. "Against whatever's actually happening over there."

"Three of us, plus Miriam, plus whatever local allies she's cultivated," Ilsa corrected. "We're not going in blind, and we're not going in alone. Just carefully."

"Carefully. Right." Petra didn't sound fully reassured, flipping through the brief's pages a second time as if additional information might materialize on a closer look. "Do we know anything more about the scale of this instability?"

"Only what Miriam's reported, and her own instruments are old, decades out of proper calibration. We'll know more once we're actually there." Ilsa checked the small kit they'd been allotted — instruments, medical supplies, nothing that would draw undue attention on a world she understood to be far more paranoid about strangers than Home's own currently was. "I want us focused on assessment first. Understanding the shape of this before we make any recommendations back to the Council."

"And if it's worse than we think?"

"Then we report that too, honestly, and let Home decide what additional resources we actually need to commit." Ilsa met Petra's eyes, holding the look until she was certain the point had properly landed. "We are not going to repeat old mistakes. If this requires more than three people, we ask for more people. Pride cost us everything once. I won't let it cost us again, not on my watch."

Dov, quiet through most of this, finally spoke, turning his own copy of the brief over in his hands. "Do you think Miriam's family will actually trust us? Given how long she's been disconnected from the mission?"

"I don't know," Ilsa admitted. "I know she chose transparency this time, when she easily could have chosen silence again. That has to count for something."

"I hope so. I'd like this to go well. Not just for the mission. For her, too. It sounds like she's spent a long time carrying this alone."

"That's a generous thought," Ilsa said, something in her expression softening slightly. "Hold onto it. We'll need generous thoughts, probably, more than we'll need instruments, before this is finished."

Ilsa closed the kit, the weight of the assignment settling properly now that preparation had given way to the actual reality of what came next. "We leave within the week. Get what rest you can. Once we're through, I doubt any of us will get much of it for a while."

Petra hesitated at the door on her way out. "Ilsa. For what it's worth — I trust your read on this. If you say careful is the right approach, I believe you."

"Thank you. I'll try to earn that, the whole way through."

"You already have. That's why I said yes to this in the first place."

The three of them dispersed to their final preparations after that, each carrying, in their own way, the weight of representing a civilization that had already lost almost everything once, now venturing back out into a wider, stranger reality than any of them had ever expected to actually witness firsthand — a small, careful team, stepping forward on behalf of people who could not afford, this time, for anything to go wrong.

✳

Chapter 138: Chapter 138

Faith and Willow both came this time, all three of them wanting one more genuine break before the household's schedule filled entirely with visiting dignitaries from three separate realities.

The world was cypress swamp, moss hanging thick from ancient trees, water dark and still except where something occasionally broke the surface and vanished again before any of them could get a clear look.

"Okay, this one's atmospheric," Faith said, peering into the shadowed water. "Very haunted-swamp energy."

"Very haunted-swamp energy that's apparently home to something genuinely interesting," Willow said, spotting movement along the nearest bank. "Look."

Turtles, dozens of them, sunning themselves on half-submerged logs, shells patterned in shades of green and brown that made them nearly invisible against the water until they moved.

"Those are incredible," Willow said, already reaching for her camera. "I need every single shell pattern documented."

"You've got time," Faith said, grinning. "They don't look like they're going anywhere fast."

They spent the trip working the swamp's edges carefully, mindful of the dark water and whatever occasionally broke its surface, and by the third evening had settled into the easy, appreciative rhythm that came from a trip asking nothing complicated of any of them.

"This is exactly what I needed," Faith said, watching fireflies begin to flicker among the cypress trees as evening settled in, leaning into Willow's side without quite meaning to. "After everything coming up. Feels good, having one more quiet one before it all starts."

"Yeah," Willow agreed, letting her stay there, an arm coming around Faith's shoulders easily. "Same, honestly. Feels like we've all earned it."

"You've definitely earned it," Xander said. "Both of you. Feels like everyone's been carrying something extra lately, one way or another."

"We all have," Faith said. "That's kind of the theme of this whole year, honestly."

They made it home with turtle photos, firefly-lit memories, and a fresh reminder for all three of them why the ordinary parts of this strange life mattered every bit as much as the extraordinary ones currently converging on them.

Buffy kissed Faith hello, then Willow, then stood back to look them both over. "Firefly light looks good on you two. Good one?"

"Zero complications," Faith confirmed, still a little pleased and a little shy about the greeting even now. "Turtles, fireflies. Exactly what we needed before everything else lands."

"Okay, wait." Willow held up a hand, something calculating crossing her face, the old notebook-instinct surfacing even now. "That's three in a row. Xander's hills, the heather one, now this. Three trips, right before the biggest week any of us have ever had, and every single one of them just happened to be exactly, precisely the kind of calm we actually needed. That's not a coincidence pattern. That's a pattern pattern."

"You think the jump's doing it on purpose," Xander said slowly. "Like the drawn-together thing, but smaller. Quieter."

"I don't know what I think yet. I think I want to start actually tracking it instead of just noticing it after the fact and feeling weird about it for five minutes." Willow was already reaching for the notebook that lived permanently in her bag at this point. "Could still be nothing. Three data points isn't a trend, Giles would be the first to tell me that. But it's not nothing, either."

"Add it to the list," Buffy said, fond, no doubt at all in her voice that Willow would. "Glad you got it, regardless of who or what's responsible," she added to Faith, already steering them both toward the kitchen before Willow could disappear into the notebook entirely.

✳

138.5. Chapter 138.5

Alan took two weeks to actually assemble what he wanted for the seal trip, which surprised Xander a little, given how casually Willow had floated it back in the doorway that day.

"You said someday," Alan pointed out, reviewing a checklist that already ran to a second page. "Someday arrived. I've had a marine vet on standby for a week now, and she's not cheap, so someday's happening before my budget notices."

"Fair. I did say someday." Xander glanced over the list — sedation protocol adapted from real-world seal rescue work, a padded transport sling instead of a crate, wet towels and a cooler of seawater to keep the animal's skin from drying out on the trip back. "This is different from the usual setup."

"Everything about a marine mammal is different from the usual setup. You can't just tranq it and haul it in a cage — skin dries out, they overheat on land faster than you'd think, and if you get the dose even slightly wrong it can affect their breathing in the water before it ever affects anything else. Dr. Okonkwo's very specific about the margins." Alan tapped the second page. "Which is why she's coming in person this time instead of just phoning in numbers."

Willow had brought her original sketches from the cliff-colony trip, spot pattern annotated in three different colors of ink, and spent most of the planning call walking Dr. Okonkwo through exactly where on the beach the colony hauled out and how steep the path down actually was.

"They're skittish about the water side, not the land side," Willow said. "Nothing came at them from the cliffs the whole time we were there. If anything's going to spook them, it's someone approaching from the surf."

"Good to know. We'll come in from the rocks, then, not the shoreline." Dr. Okonkwo made a note of her own, brisk and economical. "I want eyes on the colony a full day before anyone commits to a shot, confirm which individual we're actually after."

They went as a full group this time — Xander, Buffy, and Willow, plus Dr. Okonkwo and two of Alan's most experienced handlers, the largest crew any single trip had ever assembled outside the mastodon and sabertooth runs.

The chalk cliffs looked exactly the way Willow remembered them, gulls wheeling overhead in the same loud, indifferent numbers, and the colony was still there on the narrow beach below, spotted seal hauled out among a dozen plainer gray ones, easy to pick out even from the clifftop.

"That's her," Willow said, checking her old sketches against the animal below. "Same pattern. I'd know those spots anywhere."

They spent the first day exactly as promised, simply watching — Dr. Okonkwo tracking the colony's rhythm, when they fed, when they hauled out to rest, how close a human could get before the whole group startled back toward the water.

"She's a loner within the group," Dr. Okonkwo observed, on the second morning, watching the spotted seal settle a deliberate distance from the others. "Which actually helps us. Less risk of spooking the whole colony if we take her cleanly."

The shot itself went almost anticlimactically well, once the actual attempt came — a single dart from the rocks above, the seal barely reacting before the sedation took hold, no panicked scramble for the water the way Dr. Okonkwo had warned might happen if the dose ran even slightly high.

"Vitals are steady," she called, already moving down the path with two of Alan's handlers close behind her, the padded sling unrolling between them. "Get the sling under her before she's fully out, we want her weight distributed evenly the second she stops holding it herself."

It took all six of them working the narrow path to get the sling secured and the animal moved, careful of the loose chalk underfoot, Willow keeping a running commentary of measurements the whole way that Dr. Okonkwo actually seemed grateful for rather than annoyed by.

"Good work," Dr. Okonkwo said, once the seal was settled in the cooler, wet towels arranged and her breathing checked one more time. "That's about as clean as a first attempt on a species nobody's ever handled gets."

"We had good data going in," Xander said. "Willow's spot pattern sketches basically told you where to look before you even got here."

"They did. I'll want copies for the actual paper, if she's willing."

"Extremely willing," Willow confirmed, already looking a little like she might cry about it.

The pull took all five of them and one very confused, very sedated seal home together, and Alan's team had a proper aquatic holding tank ready and waiting by the time they came through, cool water and everything, the transition handled with a speed that left Dr. Okonkwo visibly impressed despite herself.

Faith opened the door, took one look at the whole group — soaked, chalk-dusted, trailing two of Alan's handlers still hauling gear — and did a slow, deliberate double take. "Do I even want to ask?"

"Really good one," Buffy confirmed. "We have a seal now. An actual, real, new-to-science seal. Alan's got a whole tank for her."

"Okay, that's — we just have seals now? That's a thing we do?"

"Apparently," Xander said, grinning despite the exhaustion. "Willow called it back in the doorway weeks ago. Guess someday finally showed up."

✳

Chapter 139: Chapter 139

Faith waited until the house had gone fully quiet, well past midnight on a Tuesday, before she let herself actually reach for it, Buffy's door closed down the hall, Joyce's light long since off, just the two of them — her and the hammer, propped in the corner where it had lived for months now — in the hush that the house only ever got this late, no footsteps, no TV noise, just the faint tick of the water heater and the sound of her own breathing filling the room.

She'd asked Buffy earlier that evening if it'd be weird, borrowing some alone time with it, and Buffy had just shrugged and said it's not really mine to gatekeep, F, go for it — which had somehow made this easier and harder in equal measure. The hammer sat exactly where it always sat, propped against the closet door, ordinary in every way except the one that mattered, streetlight filtering thin through the blinds and catching along its worn handle.

"Okay," she said, to the hammer, which didn't respond, because it was a hammer. "It's been a while. Figured I owed you another shot, given everything."

She thought about Thor's words, turning them over one more time before committing. Not permanent. A pause, waiting for something to catch up to itself. She'd spent months since then quietly becoming someone she trusted more than the girl who'd first reached for this thing and found it immovable — steadier, more settled, less defined by the gap between herself and Buffy than she used to be. She thought about the ritual, the countdown, the nine days that had taught her what it actually felt like to carry real weight for someone else's sake and not just her own survival. She thought about Joyce's kitchen table, and patrol nights that had stopped feeling like proving grounds and started feeling like just, simply, home.

She reached out and closed her hand around the handle.

It lifted.

Not effortlessly, not the way Buffy had described it, but it lifted — a real, genuine weight finally yielding to her grip, shifting off the shelf and into her hand like it had simply been waiting for her to actually be ready to receive it. Her arm dipped slightly with the actual weight of it before she adjusted, catching the balance properly, the metal cool against her palm despite the room's ordinary temperature.

Faith stood there a long moment, just holding it, feeling something settle in her chest that she didn't immediately have words for. Not triumph exactly. More like relief — a question finally getting an honest answer instead of just more waiting.

"Huh," she said, out loud, testing the weight of it properly now. "Huh, okay. There it is."

She didn't feel radically different. That was the strange part — no flash of light, no cosmic recognition, just an ordinary hammer sitting in her hand, considerably lighter than it looked, exactly the way Buffy had once described it. She turned it over once, catching the worn grain of the handle in the streetlight, and found herself, unexpectedly, laughing — short, disbelieving, the sound echoing a little too loud in the sleeping house.

'Guess Thor was right,' she thought, setting it back down carefully, not quite ready to actually take it anywhere yet, just needing to know it was possible. 'Wasn't ever about being good enough. Just about catching up to myself.' It felt like an important thing to have learned, even without fully understanding the mechanism behind it.

She stayed cross-legged on her bedroom floor a while longer in the near-dark, the hammer resting beside her, letting the whole strange, quiet victory settle properly before she trusted herself to move on to whatever came next.

She called Buffy that night, needing to tell someone, and found herself smiling the whole time she explained it, pacing her room with the phone pressed to her ear.

"You lifted it," Buffy said, delighted. "Faith, that's huge."

"Yeah. Feels huge. Doesn't feel as huge as I thought it would, though, if that makes sense. More just — quiet. Settled."

"That tracks, actually. Good things settling instead of exploding feels like progress, honestly."

"Yeah," Faith agreed. "Yeah, I think it does."

"You gonna tell Thor?"

"Eventually. Wanna keep it to myself a little longer first. Feels like mine to hold onto for a minute before I hand it over to somebody else's reaction."

"That's fair. Take your time."

She told him three days later, finding him alone on Joyce's porch in the same spot they'd talked once before, and simply held out her hand, palm up, waiting.

Thor looked at her a long moment, understanding immediately without needing the words spelled out. "You lifted it."

"Yeah."

"How does it feel?"

Faith considered the question properly before answering, the way he always seemed to consider things before speaking. "Quiet. Settled. Not the fireworks I expected, honestly. More like something that was already true finally getting to be true out loud."

Thor's face broke into something honestly, deeply pleased. "That is, in my experience, exactly how it should feel. The loud version is usually a warning sign, not a victory."

"Good to know. Wish somebody'd told me that going in."

"I am telling you now." He studied her a moment longer, something proud and a little emotional crossing his features despite his usual composure. "I am glad, Faith. Genuinely. I told you it was never permanent. I did not expect to be quite this pleased at being proven right so soon."

"Yeah, well. Don't get used to it. Might take me another year to prove you right about the next thing."

"I look forward to it regardless," Thor said, and meant it, and Faith found herself, sitting there on the familiar porch steps, feeling more like herself than she had in longer than she could easily measure.

She didn't tell the rest of the group right away, wanting to keep the discovery just hers a little longer first, but she found herself, over the following days, standing a little straighter, carrying herself with a little less of the old, quiet weight she'd been hauling around since the first failed attempt — not because anything external had changed, but because something internal finally had, and that, she was slowly coming to realize, had always been the actual point.

✳

Chapter 140: Chapter 140

Fury arrived without fanfare, exactly as Natasha had promised, coming through SHIELD's crossing point same as she had and finishing the trip in an unremarkable sedan that could have belonged to anyone, parked a full block away from the Magic Box for reasons nobody bothered asking about, and made his way inside unhurried, long since impossible to impress by anything short of the actually world-ending.

Xander had expected, somehow, something much more dramatic — an entourage, at minimum, or at least a car that looked like it belonged to someone important. Instead, Fury walked in alone, coat plain, expression giving away exactly nothing, and the whole shop seemed to hold its breath for a moment as everyone present recalibrated their expectations all at once.

"Director Fury," Giles said, extending a hand. "Thank you for coming."

"Wasn't really optional, once Thor's report landed on my desk." Fury's eye swept the shop once, cataloguing it the same efficient way Natasha's had weeks earlier, pausing fractionally on Xander before moving on. "Let's get to it. I've read the report. I want the parts that didn't make it into writing."

They spent the first hour walking him through everything — Xander's ability, the Slayer-ritual origin, Thor's search, the hammer, the worthy-hands mechanism Faith had only just properly resolved. Fury listened without much visible reaction, occasionally asking a question sharp enough to reveal he'd absorbed notably more of the written report than his opening comment suggested, his single eye tracking whoever was speaking with an intensity that made Xander, more than once, lose his own train of thought mid-sentence.

"You alright, son?" Fury asked, at one point, catching Xander's hesitation.

"Yeah, sorry. Just — you're kind of intense to explain things to."

"I get that a lot." Something almost like humor flickered across his expression, gone as quickly as it arrived. "Keep going. You're doing fine."

"Here's what I keep coming back to," Fury said, once they'd finished, leaning back slightly, arms crossed. "Every piece of this traces back to the same source. The kid's ability. The hammer's landing here. The readings my people are tracking, and Strange's people independently, from what I understand."

"That's our working theory as well," Giles confirmed. "The fracture reaching backward and forward simultaneously, weakening boundaries between specific realities rather than causing broad, general instability."

"Which means this town isn't a coincidence. It's a symptom." Fury's expression didn't change, but something in his voice sharpened. "I don't like symptoms without a clear read on the underlying condition."

"Nor do we," Giles admitted. "We're rather hoping additional perspectives, once they arrive, might help clarify exactly that."

"Strange and his people. And whoever's coming from the other side, through the girl's family." Fury considered that, gaze moving briefly toward the window, toward the ordinary street outside that gave no hint of the enormity of what was actually unfolding inside. "Everyone converging on the same signal, from completely different angles."

"That does seem to be the pattern, yes."

Fury was quiet a long moment, working through something, the silence stretching long enough that Willow visibly resisted the urge to fill it. "I've spent my career assuming threats come from somewhere specific. An enemy. A weapon. Something with intent behind it." He looked at Giles directly, weight settling into the question. "This doesn't have intent, does it. This is just physics, breaking."
