They spent three easy days in the meadow, cataloguing without urgency, resting without guilt, and by the final evening had eased into something that felt, despite everything they now knew about the wider stakes involved, truly, wonderfully ordinary.

"You think it's really over?" Buffy asked, watching the sunset with real peace for the first time in weeks, leaning back against Xander while Willow's head rested on his shoulder, all three of them tangled together in the easy way that had become second nature by now. "The Convergence, all of it?"

"The immediate part, yeah," Xander said. "Ilsa said the wider instability will take longer to fully settle. But the actual crisis, the part that could've ended everything? That part's done."

"Good," Buffy said, simply. "I like knowing that. Even if the bigger picture's still complicated."

"Same," Willow agreed. "Feels good, having room to just be normal again. Even knowing normal's got a much bigger context now than it used to."

They made it home steadier than they'd felt in weeks, the ordinary comfort of the trip settling into something that would carry them forward — proof, gentle and undeniable, that whatever else this strange, expanded life now included, the actual heart of it remained exactly what it had always been.

"Good one?" Joyce asked, catching them at the door — the exact same three words she'd said a hundred times before this, back when it was just a habit and not, like now, something that came out sounding almost unbearably relieved.

"Really good one," Xander confirmed. "Genuinely, wonderfully ordinary."

"I'm so glad," Joyce said, and meant it completely, pulling all three of them into a hug that lasted a beat longer than any of her usual ones did, nobody in the doorway in any hurry to point that out.

✳

Chapter 154: Chapter 154

Joyce's list marked its own quiet milestone that Sunday, the entry she read aloud carrying more weight than any single line had in longer than any of them could remember: We faced something that could have ended notably more than just our own small corner of existence, and we came through it together, stranger and more connected than we ever expected to become. I'm choosing to call that a good outcome, however it got here.

"That's a big entry," Xander said.

"It's been a big month," Joyce said, passing the potatoes down a table significantly fuller than usual — Faith, Buffy, Willow, Jesse, Cordelia, Giles, Thor, all crowded around in the comfortable chaos that had, by now, become simply how Sunday dinner worked, chairs pulled from three different rooms to accommodate everyone, the whole scene warm with the noise of a family considerably larger than the one it had started as.

"Speaking of big," Faith said, "I still can't get over how casual everyone's being about the whole 'we prevented reality from unraveling' thing. Feels like that should be a bigger deal at this table specifically."

"We process big things by making them ordinary," Willow said. "It's basically the whole Sunnydale coping strategy."

"Deeply validating information about my adopted hometown," Thor said, entirely serious, and the table dissolved into the easy laughter that had become, over the past several months, its own kind of ritual.

Jesse, who'd spent most of dinner quietly working through his own version of processing, finally spoke up. "Can we talk about the fact that I've now met an actual Avenger, an actual sorcerer, and people from an fully different reality, and somehow none of that beats the napkin thing as my most embarrassing Sunnydale memory?"

"Nothing beats the napkin thing," Cordelia said, delighted. "That's just objectively true."

"I stand by my earlier defense. It was a small collection."

"It's never gonna stop being funny, Jesse. Make your peace with that."

Cordelia, who'd been folded into enough of the actual details by now to follow most of the conversation, finally spoke up herself. "Okay, but for real, is it actually over? Or is this just, like, round one?"

"Ilsa said the immediate crisis is resolved," Giles said. "The wider instability across affected realities will take far longer to fully settle, and may require ongoing monitoring for years. But the acute danger, the part that threatened genuine catastrophe — that's passed."

"So we might have to do this again someday."

"Possibly. Though hopefully with much more warning next time, and notably less immediate peril."

"I'll take that trade," Faith said, and the table murmured general agreement.

"For what it's worth," Buffy added, "I think we've earned a honestly long stretch of boring after this one. Universe owes us that much, at minimum."

"I would not count on the universe honoring debts," Thor said, "but I appreciate the optimism regardless."

Later, on the porch, Xander found himself sitting beside Faith, her hand loosely laced with his, both of them looking out at a street that felt, for the first time in longer than either could easily place, completely, wonderfully ordinary — porch lights flickering on up and down the block, a dog barking somewhere distant, the whole unremarkable rhythm of a town that had no idea how close it had come to something it would never have understood even if it had. Through the window behind them, he could still hear Buffy and Willow's laughter mixing with the general noise of dinner winding down, and something in his chest settled at the sound of it, complete in a way he hadn't quite let himself name out loud yet.

"Good month," Faith said, eventually.

"Good month," he agreed. "Feels weird to say that, given everything. But yeah. Good month." He pressed a kiss to her temple, the gesture long since settled into something easy and unremarkable after months of practice.

"Maybe complicated things are allowed to be good, even when they're also enormous and scary."

"That's annoyingly wise."

"I have my moments."

"That's still your line, though, right? Not somebody borrowing it?"

"Damn right it's mine. You people can borrow the multitudes thing. This one's staying with me."

They sat together a while longer, the evening settling into comfortable quiet, and Xander found himself turning over, one more time, everything the past year had actually held — four years of solitary jumps becoming something shared, a mystery that had turned into a family, a god who'd become a friend, a crisis that had, against every reasonable fear, resolved into something survivable.

'Big life,' he thought, watching the ordinary street settle into evening dark. 'Bigger than I ever expected, back when this was just me and a random world every weekend.' It felt, sitting here now, less like an ending and more like exactly what it actually was — one more quiet chapter, closing gently, in a story that would, he suspected, keep finding new and impossible ways to grow.

"Same time next weekend?" Faith asked, nodding toward the horizon, toward whatever ordinary, extraordinary world waited next.

"Same time next weekend," Xander confirmed, and meant it completely, ready, as he always somehow managed to be, for whatever came next.

Bonus Chapter: The Honest Trailer

The screen showed Xander Harris standing behind Sunnydale High’s football field inside a square marked by traffic cones.

“In a world where most teenagers spend the weekend sleeping late, avoiding homework, and making regrettable social decisions, one teenager has a standing appointment with an uncontrollable interdimensional wilderness commute.”

The cones vanished. So did Sunnydale.

“Same time next weekend.”

This is Working for the Weekend: Bridge Over Troubled Water.

Watch Xander get pulled once a week into a random, uninhabited version of Earth for three subjective days, with no control over the destination, no guarantee of ever returning, and a backpack whose inventory suggests several previous weekends ended with highly specific new rules.

Water purifier. Machete. Collapsible fish tank. Tranquilizer pistol. Bear mace. Air horn. Portable shed.

“That isn’t camping gear,” the narrator said. “That’s a patch history.”

Follow him across untouched grasslands, forests, deserts, and coastlines while he catalogs extinct animals, retrieves impossible specimens, and learns that “random world every week” is less a superpower than an extremely aggressive continuing-education program in field biology.

Watch a giant ground sloth arrive behind the football field and become several adults’ problem with no advance notice.

Meet Buffy and Willow, who eventually join the jumps, turning Xander’s solitary exile into a three-person relationship, expedition team, and recurring reminder that the phrase “family camping trip” can include prehistoric megafauna.

Meet Joyce, whose contribution to interdimensional exploration consists of hugs, food, spare rooms, and the firm conviction that every apocalypse is easier to manage after everybody has eaten.

Meet Faith in a highway motel after Diana’s death, where Joyce offers her a room without a clipboard, Council terms, or a demand for gratitude.

The narrator paused on the doorway.

“The story contains multiversal collapse, gods, portals, alien technology, and a hammer. One of its most important acts is still a woman asking a frightened girl whether she wants somewhere safer to sleep. We’re not making that smaller.”

Then watch the crossover radius expand.

Meet Davy, Millie, and Cent Rice on the one wildside world Xander unexpectedly reaches twice. Meet Charlie and the fixed wooden gate. Meet Miriam and the civilization called Home. Meet Thor, Natasha, Fury, Tony, Steve, Bruce, Strange, Wong, and enough different Earths that the word “home” requires capitalization rules.

The door in Colorado is literal wood with hinges and a padlock. The emergency Gate is a luminous tear in reality. Tony’s relay piggybacks on residual dimensional signatures. Xander’s weekly pull does whatever it wants.

“Please consult the travel appendix,” the narrator said. “No, not that door. The other door.”

Watch Tony Stark respond to a cross-reality communication problem by building a device that rides the wake of someone else’s portal, then scale it up enough to drive a minivan through.

Ninety percent confidence.

“The other ten percent was apparently ‘we become a continuity error.’”

Then face the Convergence: realities destabilizing, Home reconnecting, the whole expanding family discovering the wildside mechanism is part of something much larger, older, and less accidental than a weird weekend curse.

Miriam’s team crosses. Xander carries more people through his personal jump than he has ever tried. Strange helps keep existence from becoming an optional setting. Everyone survives, then immediately schedules another ordinary trip because this series refuses to let cosmic scale take the ordinary heart away from it.

## The Bridge Over Troubled Water Drinking Game

Take a drink every time Xander returns with an animal no local veterinarian was trained to treat.

Take two whenever somebody says “long story” and is forced to provide the long version before dawn.

Drink whenever a new world requires clarification about which side is tame, wild, Home, or MCU.

Take a sip whenever Joyce asks whether it was a good trip.

Finish your drink when Tony drives a minivan between realities on ninety-percent confidence.

Do not drink every time a chapter number contains a decimal. The interstitial POV structure is deliberate; your hospitalization would not be.

## Starring

Xander Harris as Wildside Gig Economy Employee of the Month.

Buffy and Willow as Two Girls, One Random Dimensional Pull.

Joyce Summers as Bed, Breakfast, and Apocalypse Management.

Faith as No Clipboard, No Council, One Spare Room.

Davy, Millie, and Cent as The Other Teleporting Family You Meet While Teleporting.

Charlie as The Boy Behind the Correct Wooden Door.

Tony Stark as Interdimensional Minivan Engineer.

Thor as Residual Signature Donor.

Doctor Strange as Continuity Repair Wizard.

And Xander’s backpack as Mary Poppins’ Tactical Field Kit.

Alternate titles appeared:

Into the Wildside-Verse.

The Weekend Has No Cancellation Policy.

One Hundred Fifty-Four Chapters and a Very Full Kitchen.

The narrator looked at the title already printed on the manuscript.

“Simon and Garfunkel did the naming work for us.”

The card appeared.

# BRIDGE OVER TROUBLED WATER

Directed by: a weekly schedule with no regard for emergencies.

Written by: every crossover door somebody forgot to close.

Field support by: a backpack containing the exact consequence of last week’s mistake.

Special thanks to: Joyce’s front door, which stayed open even when reality did not.

The last screen showed Xander, Buffy, and Willow in an uncomplicated meadow after the crisis.

“Good one?” Joyce asked when they returned.

“Really good one.”

The narrator let the ordinary answer stand.

“Same time next weekend.”
